After observing $y$, update the log odds by $\ln(q_1(y\mid x)/q_0(y\mid x))$, producing posterior $u$. Then the regime persists with probability $1-h$ and refreshes to Bernoulli $\pi$ with probability $0<h\le1$, $0<\pi<1$. The new prior is $g(u)=(1-h)u+h\pi$. A rounded filter stores log odds on a regular grid of spacing $\Delta$ from $\operatorname{logit}(h\pi)$ to $\operatorname{logit}(1-h(1-\pi))$. Start with $\pi$, rounding it on the same grid. For $h=1$ the exact prior is constant and needs no carry.

Set $a=g(1)$ and $b=g(0)$. In log odds the transition is $\ln[(a e^s+b)/((1-a)e^s+1-b)]$. Maximizing its derivative gives the Lipschitz constant
$$
\tau=\frac{\sqrt{a(1-b)}-\sqrt{b(1-a)}}{\sqrt{a(1-b)}+\sqrt{b(1-a)}}<1.
$$
Indeed the denominator of the derivative divided by $e^s$ is minimized at $e^{2s}=b(1-b)/(a(1-a))$. Observing the same word adds the same log likelihood ratio to both filters. Thus rounding to the nearest grid point gives prior log-odds discrepancy at most $\Delta/[2(1-\tau)]$ at every release. The derivative of $\ln((1-w)q_0(y)+wq_1(y))$ with respect to prior log odds is the posterior of regime one minus $w$, of absolute value at most one. On **any** length-$T$ transcript the sum of absolute conditional log-score discrepancies is therefore at most $T\Delta/[2(1-\tau)]$. Positive HMM contraction and its role in predictive memory are established techniques. [Atar and Zeitouni on filter stability](https://www.wisdom.weizmann.ac.il/~zeitouni/pdf/diffusionsrev.pdf) · [Feng and Meng, 2026 preprint](https://arxiv.org/html/2611.13760v1#S3)

A sharper expected-loss guarantee accounts for information spent on the actual words. Work under the exact learned source. Let $V$ be Bernoulli KL from the exact prior to the rounded filter's prior. The joint KL chain rule for regime and word gives $V=d+E D$, where $d$ is the KL of the issued word laws and $D$ is the Bernoulli KL of the two posteriors after that word. This holds for arbitrary positive raw-word emissions. After applying the same refresh, convexity bounds the new prior KL by $(1-h)D$. Let $v=g(u)$ be the exact new prior and $\hat v$ be the new approximate prior before rounding. Perturbing its natural logit by $\epsilon$ adds at most $(\hat v-v)\epsilon+\epsilon^2/8$ to KL, using the Bernoulli log-partition Hessian bound $1/4$. Pinsker gives $|\hat v-v|\le(1-h)\sqrt{D/2}$. With $|\epsilon|\le\Delta/2$, Young's inequality absorbs $hD$, leaving
$$
V_{\rm next}\le D+\frac{\Delta^2}{32}\left(1+\frac{(1-h)^2}{h}\right).
$$
The initial KL is at most $\Delta^2/32$. Telescoping the chain rules bounds total expected transcript KL by $T\Delta^2[1+(1-h)^2/h]/32\le T\Delta^2/(32h)$. If each update independently rounds to adjacent grid points with conditional mean error zero, the first-order term averages to zero and the conditional variance is at most $\Delta^2/4$. The resulting expected operational KL is at most $T\Delta^2/32$, including the initial rounding. This version consumes randomness. It scores the predictor using its realized carry, with observations generated independently by the exact source. Actual held-out language can favor a coarse rounding or penalize it. The expectation bound requires the specified source; the absolute pathwise bound holds on the observed language tape.

For the word-class implementation, let $r$ be the base mass of a learned set $S$. State $i$ multiplies the odds of $S$ by $e^{\beta_i}$, keeping the conditional word distributions within and outside $S$. On target word $y$ its raw-word likelihood ratio to the base is $e^{\beta_i 1_S(y)}/[1+(e^{\beta_i}-1)r]$. All filter parameters can therefore be fitted using ordinary next-word log loss, the base mass and the membership of the word actually observed. A single probability carries the full posterior between releases. On the same output set a conventional competitor can carry an exponentially averaged signed error $1_S(y)-r$. This is exactly the negative derivative of the conditional log loss with respect to an odds shift at zero. The held-out comparison determines which state better predicts subsequent words. [Native fits and normalized update](word_persistence.c) · [Chronological comparison](text_persistence.py)

## Which prediction errors justify constructing a recurrent direction?

**PROVED for a frozen positive base law and predictable linear features.** Partition the raw vocabulary into $d$ classes, and let $q_t$ be the base model's class probabilities given the actual past. At target time $t$ let $e_t=\mathbf{1}_{c(y_t)}-q_t$ denote the class one-hot error. Take any square-integrable feature vector $u_t$ computed before $y_t$ from the actual observed history and the frozen model. For a matrix $W$ apply log-odds shifts $z_t=Wu_t$ to the classes, multiplying every raw-word probability by $\exp(z_{t,c})/\sum_jq_{t,j}\exp(z_{t,j})$. The resulting normalized law preserves the full distribution inside each class. Expectations below may mean either the empirical average on named training positions or a specified sequence distribution. Those choices give different statements.

Let $C=E[e_tu_t^\top]$ and $S=E[u_tu_t^\top]+\lambda I$ with $\lambda>0$. For every $W$, its mean raw-word log-score gain $G(W)$ satisfies
$$
G(W)-\frac{\lambda}{4}\|W\|_F^2\ \ge\
\langle W,C\rangle-\frac14\operatorname{tr}(WSW^\top).
$$
Indeed the exact gain per word is $z_{t,c(y_t)}-\ln\sum_jq_{t,j}e^{z_{t,j}}$. For the random variable with probabilities $q_{t,j}$ and values $z_{t,j}$, the log moment-generating function above its mean is at most its squared range divided by eight, by integrating the tilted variance bound. The squared range is at most $2\|z_t\|_2^2$. The coefficient $1/4$ cannot be reduced uniformly, since a fair binary base with shifts $(v,-v)$ gives $\ln\cosh v\sim v^2/2$. The multinomial log-likelihood quadratic bound is classical. [Böhning, multinomial logistic regression](https://www.ism.ac.jp/editsec/aism/pdf/044_1_0197.pdf)

Write $A=CS^{-1/2}$. Completing the square gives the certificate $\|A\|_F^2-\|WS^{1/2}-2A\|_F^2/4$. Its maximum among matrices of rank at most $k$ is $\sum_{j\le k}\sigma_j(A)^2$, attained at $W=2A_kS^{-1/2}$ for a best rank-$k$ singular truncation $A_k$. For a singular unregularized $S$ the same statement uses the inverse square root on its range, since $Cv=0$ for $v$ in its nullspace. The class-constant component has zero contrast because $\mathbf{1}^\top C=0$. Ties at the cutoff can leave multiple equally valid directions. Jensen's inequality also gives $G(W)\le\langle W,C\rangle$, so $C=0$ is necessary and sufficient for absence of *any* strict gain in this linear-tilt family on the declared distribution. Any nonzero $C$ already permits a rank-one improvement.

For the prior history of errors $u_{t+1}=(1-\rho)u_t+\rho e_t$, a rank-one fit $W=b a^\top$ actually deploys with just one dynamic value. Its update is
$$
s_{t+1}=(1-\rho)s_t+\rho[a_{c(y_t)}-a^\top q_t],\qquad z_t=b\,s_t.
$$
Thus ordinary errors can specify both which scalar to remember and which outputs to modify. The supplied class partition, common scalar decay family, base normalization and matrix rank remain explicit inductive choices. The deployed predictor must have access to $q_t$ to compute $a^\top q_t$ and the new normalizer. An arbitrary vector transition does not admit this scalar reduction unless the fitted input direction closes under that transition on reachable errors. Using the corrected predictor's own errors would also change the training objective. Neural language caches offer a separate history-dependent conventional construction. [Grave, Joulin and Usunier](https://openreview.net/pdf?id=B184E5qee) · [Spectral acquisition and normalized fit](direction_learning.py)

Positive training gain can occur under pure noise. One fair binary target, a constant feature one and no regularizer give fitted shifts $(1,-1)$ oriented toward that observation. The observed gain is $1-\ln\cosh1$, and the next independent fair target has expected gain $-\ln\cosh1$. Consequently an empirically certified direction must still earn its price on future raw words. For held-out sequence evaluation, the product of its conditional likelihood ratios to the base has mean one if the base generates the sequence and the frozen correction is causal. This supplies a null test against that particular base model. Interpreting an improvement on one chronological tape as a population claim requires further assumptions.

## How many scalar error coordinates does a normalized predictor need?

**PROVED for common decay and real-linear realization of class contrasts.** There are $d$ exhaustive classes. The observed one-hot minus the base probabilities lies in $H=\{v:\mathbf1^\top v=0\}$. Starting from zero, a shared-rate error average $u$ remains in $H$. Let $P=I-\mathbf1\mathbf1^\top/d$. A normalized tilt by $Wu$ depends on it only through $PWu$. The class probabilities and the known positive base determine $PWu$ by taking the centered log ratio. Hence the least number of real linear history coordinates needed by a linear encoder and a linear readout of log contrasts is $\operatorname{rank}(PWP)$ when attainable error histories span $H$ and those contrasts must be realized. A rank factorization supplies an encoder and a readout of that size. The common leak lets the encoder update using the projected new error. Conversely any factorization through $k$ coordinates has rank at most $k$ on the span. If arbitrary nonlinear readout is allowed, the same lower bound holds for a linear encoder required to handle an open subset of $H$ at a common public base law, by taking differences in its kernel. Spanning by a finite collection of points alone gives no such nonlinear lower bound.

For $W=\operatorname{diag}(\gamma)$ with exactly $k<d$ nonzero coefficients, the rank is $k$. If $P W u=0$ then $Wu$ is constant. A zero diagonal position forces that constant to be zero, leaving $d-k-1$ free dimensions among the omitted coordinates of $u\in H$. For $k=d$, all possible kernel vectors have $u_i=c/\gamma_i$. The rank is $d-1$ unless $\sum_i 1/\gamma_i=0$, in which case it is $d-2$. In particular all 33 unrounded accumulators of the original diagonal fit can always be evaluated using at most 32 real coordinates. This algebra alone does not settle how many bits suffice at a given loss on the word distribution actually encountered.

**Exact cost of omitting coordinates.** For fixed fitted slopes and ordinary pre-observation histories let $z_{ti}=\gamma_i u_{ti}$ and $a_{ti}=q_{ti}(\exp z_{ti}-1)$. If only $S$ is installed, the extra NLL on a recorded tape is
$$
L(S)=\sum_t\ln\!\left(1+\sum_{i\in S}a_{ti}\right)
-\sum_{i\in S}\sum_{t:c(y_t)=i}z_{ti}.
$$
Each coordinate still updates from its own base probability, independently of which others are installed. Thus fitting and subset comparison can reuse the same precomputed raw error histories without changing the intervening observations. For two added coordinates with changes $a,b$ and previous normalizer $X>0$, the mixed loss difference is $\ln[X(X+a+b)/((X+a)(X+b))]$. Its sign reverses with $ab$. For instance with a uniform three-class base, multiplying two distinct classes by two gives the ratio $15/16$. Multiplying the first by two and the second by one half gives $21/20$. Thus an exact per-coordinate sum, or one universal diminishing-returns guarantee, cannot replace the normalization calculation. The finite procedure compares exact normalized training scores, checks the smallest support sizes exhaustively, and selects by subsequent loss with an explicit packet charge. [Subset construction and scoring](sparse_directions.py) · [Frozen choices](sparse_selection.json)

## What does a predictor lose when it can sample its base but cannot read its probabilities?

**PROVED for sampled error accumulation on a fixed observable tape.** Let $q_{t,i}$ be the frozen base probability of active class $i$ before seeing word $y_t$. With $x_{t,i}=1_{\{c(y_t)=i\}}$, define $s_{t+1,i}=(1-\rho)s_{t,i}+\rho(x_{t,i}-q_{t,i})$ from zero, with $0<\rho\le1$. Add logit shifts $\gamma_i s_{t,i}$ on active classes and zero elsewhere, preserving relative probabilities within each class. A sampled update uses $b_t\ge1$ independent raw base draws at time $t$, replacing $q_{t,i}$ by its sample fraction. The evaluator still computes the full normalized reported law. Conditional on an arbitrary fixed tape, sample independently across times. Then $E(\hat s_{t,i}-s_{t,i})=0$. Its coordinate variance starts at zero and obeys
$$
V_{t+1,i}=(1-\rho)^2V_{t,i}+\rho^2q_{t,i}(1-q_{t,i})/b_t.
$$
The same raw samples can be shared across classes. No independence between different coordinates is needed.

At a given time the active logit errors have mean zero. The expected observed-word NLL gap is exactly the expected forward KL from the exact-state predictive law to the sampled-state law. The difference between the two expressions is the mean of a linear logit-error term, which is zero under both the fixed observed word and the exact-state predictive law. This identity holds even when the external tape was produced by a different source. Expanding the log normalizer around the exact logits, the centered log moment-generating term is at most a quarter of the squared Euclidean logit error, by the range bound above. Thus expected extra NLL over any reporting positions $\mathcal T$ is between zero and $\tfrac14\sum_{t\in\mathcal T,i}\gamma_i^2V_{t,i}$. This interval is conditional on the fixed tape and concerns unrounded state. With fixed $b$, the worst upper order is $O(T\rho\sum_i\gamma_i^2/b)$ for $T$ reports. For one active class and constant base mass $1/2$, the log normalizer has curvature at least $\sigma(|\gamma|)(1-\sigma(|\gamma|))$ in logit coordinates since the error averages lie in $[-1,1]$. Taylor's lower bound yields at least half this constant times $\gamma^2\sum V_t$. Here $V_t=\rho[1-(1-\rho)^{2(t-1)}]/[4b(2-\rho)]$. This gives matching order for the specified sampled-update algorithm when $T\rho$ is large and $\gamma\ne0$. A different estimator supplied with a known constant base could exploit that knowledge.

For a fixed state, raw-word **generation** from its normalized tilted law is possible using only a base sampler. Write $z(y)$ for the shift, $M=\max_y e^{z(y)}$, and $Z=E_{Y\sim q}e^{z(Y)}$. Draw a raw word from the base and accept with probability $e^{z(y)}/M$, repeating until accepted. One trial accepts $y$ with probability $q(y)e^{z(y)}/M$; summing over preceding failures gives precisely $q(y)e^{z(y)}/Z$. The expected number of base proposals is $M/Z$. Another independent draw, if purchased, estimates the base expectation for updating the next state. That extra draw influences future reports. On a fixed exogenous tape the first rejection proposal also has the base law and can be reused. On generated continuations it can be correlated with the accepted word. The measured generator pays for an independent update draw to implement its declared joint law. The external evaluator can still access full probabilities to validate them. [Native update and risk replay](sample_access.c) · [Exact small examples and generated-word loop](sample_access.py)

**Further consequence under historical replay access.** Suppose the aim is exact sampling from the *unrounded probability-read update itself* and a raw base sampler can revisit arbitrary past contexts. Write $w_j=\rho(1-\rho)^{t-1-j}$ for $1\le j<t$, $W_t=\sum w_j$, and $H_i=\sum w_jx_{j,i}$. For $\gamma_i\ge0$, the unnormalized multiplicative odds at time $t$ are $e^{\gamma_iH_i}e^{-\gamma_i\sum_jw_jq_{j,i}}$. The second factor is an exact success probability using only historical draws. Draw $L\sim\operatorname{Poisson}(\gamma_iW_t)$. Independently for each of its marks choose $j$ with probability $w_j/W_t$ and query the base at the historical context $j$. Succeed if no returned word belongs to $i$. Poisson thinning gives the stated exponential probability. For $\gamma_i<0$, use the complementary event instead, with deterministic multiplier $e^{\gamma_i H_i+|\gamma_i|W_t}$. At the first word the empty history contributes a factor of one. Choose an envelope $M$ covering one and all deterministic multipliers. A proposal of class $i$ passes its deterministic multiplier test and then its independent Poisson test. A proposal from outside passes with probability $1/M$. Every trial accepts a raw word with the exact desired unnormalized mass divided by $M$, giving the ideal normalized output after repetition. With $|\gamma_i|\le\Gamma$, the expected total base draws per accepted word are at most $(1+\Gamma)e^{2\Gamma}$, a uniform worst-case bound that can be very loose. This derivation prices historical re-query access; it grants neither a finite old-context store nor access to the original Transformer state for free. The measured 512-word run below implements the different sampled-update rule with its four-byte carry.

**Scoring and learning without the base normalizer.** For a fixed installed state, if $J$ proposals are required by the rejection sampler then $\Pr(J>k)=(1-Z/M)^k$. With $H_0=0$ and $H_n=\sum_{k=1}^n1/k$, $E H_{J-1}=\sum_{k\ge1}(1-Z/M)^k/k=-\ln(Z/M)$. Thus $\ln M-H_{J-1}-z(y_t)$ is an unbiased estimate of the relative NLL on the actual observed word. This is an application of inverse-binomial likelihood estimation to the normalization of a fitted correction. Absolute base NLL is a separate quantity. The accepted class indicator minus the observed one, multiplied by the state value, is an unbiased gradient in that class's gain at the realized state. When state updates use only raw observations and independent base samples and do not depend on the gains, averaging gives the gradient of the expected sampled-state objective. [Van Opheusden, Acerbi and Ma](https://pubmed.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/33362195/)

At zero tilt an even simpler acquisition bound needs no rejection run. For any one prespecified class let $g_i=\sum_t s_{t,i}(q_{t,i}-x_{t,i})$, the **total** NLL derivative at zero. Replace both $s$ and $q$ by their sampled versions using the same $b$ independent base draws at each time. The estimate is unbiased since the pre-observation state precedes the current samples. Replacing one raw draw changes its own summand by at most $1/b$ since $|s|\le1$. All future state perturbations together affect the sum by at most $\rho\sum_{h\ge0}(1-\rho)^h/b=1/b$. Independent bounded differences and a union bound over $d$ prespecified coordinates give $|\hat g_i-g_i|\le u=\sqrt{(2T/b)\ln(2d/\eta)}$ simultaneously with probability at least $1-\eta$. Put $a_i=(|\hat g_i|-u)_+$. The single-coordinate loss Hessian is at most $T/4$ for all gains. Taking $\gamma_i=-4\operatorname{sign}(\hat g_i)a_i/T$ therefore guarantees at least $2a_i^2/T$ nats of **total exact-state training gain** jointly for the candidates, including one selected from them. This uses real state and a fixed tape. An observed zero certificate can coexist with a useful fitted correction. No conclusions about future data follow solely from this calculation.

A bounded black-box history-query budget cannot in general produce the exact ideal generative law. Already with one past context of unknown Bernoulli probability $p$, fixed nonzero gain, a nonzero update rate and a known binary base for the current word, the required success probability can be $\sigma(A-cp)$ with $c\ne0$. Any algorithm making at most $B$ draws from that past context induces a polynomial in $p$ of degree at most $B$. Pre-generate $B$ Bernoulli bits and condition on their ordered outcomes, integrating all other private randomness. No such polynomial agrees with the required nonconstant sigmoid on an interval, since equality of the real analytic functions would extend to the real line, where a bounded polynomial is constant. The historical Poisson construction uses an unbounded number of possible calls with finite mean. If the last $L$ past observations are available, truncating the ordinary leaky state changes any active coordinate by at most $(1-\rho)^L$ from zero initialization. For $|\gamma_i|\le\Gamma$, range control bounds per-word conditional KL between the resulting laws by $\Gamma^2(1-\rho)^{2L}/2$. This trades a finite context store for a specified prediction allowance.

## Which prediction errors justify a connection between different word classes?

**Proved for bounded predictable readouts.** Let $X_j=1_{\{Y=j\}}$, let $q$ be the available full-support base law, and let $\mathcal H_j$ specify the information allowed to adjust output $j$. Choose a bounded $\mathcal H_j$-measurable logit shift $z_j$. The expected normalized log gain over the base satisfies
$$
E\sum_j(X_j-q_j)z_j-\tfrac18 E(\max_j z_j-\min_j z_j)^2
\ \le\ G(z)\ \le\ E\sum_j(X_j-q_j)z_j .
$$
The upper bound is Jensen's inequality for $\log\sum q_j e^{z_j}$. The lower bound is Hoeffding's lemma applied to the logit range under the base law. Consequently **some allowed correction improves population prediction exactly when at least one conditional residual** $g_j=E[X_j-q_j\mid\mathcal H_j]$ **is nonzero with positive probability**. For sufficiency shift just that output by $4g_j$, giving at least $2E g_j^2$ nats. Necessity holds for arbitrary simultaneous shifts with the stated information. On a fixed collection of positions the same statement applies by sampling a position uniformly and using its declared information. A numerical gain statement also needs the cost of obtaining and representing the conditional mean.

For a four-symbol stationary example take $P_{ij}=1/4+(3/16)u_i v_j$ with $u=(1,-1,0,0)$ and $v=(0,0,1,-1)$. The uniform distribution is stationary, $P^2$ has every entry $1/4$, and $(P^k)_{jj}=1/4$ for every $k\ge1$. For a fixed $j$, the probability that its indicator equals one at any specified $n$ times is $4^{-n}$ by the Markov property. Inclusion–exclusion gives independence of its entire Bernoulli indicator process. Independent uniform base draws preserve this property. Even arbitrarily expressive own-class histories therefore earn at most zero expected gain over the uniform base. A single bit recording the preceding occurrence of symbol 0 can tilt symbol 2 by $\log(7/3)$ when the bit is set. It saves $\tfrac14\,\mathrm{kl}(7/16\,\|\,1/4)=0.0207527$ nats per stationary word. Exactly reproducing all next-word conditionals needs three distinguishable preceding contexts, since the chain has three distinct transition rows. [Exact arithmetic and ordinary-data controls](receipt_control.py)

**A certificate for learning the connection through samples.** Fix the text, the positive base law at each position and a finite family of $M$ possible rules. Before drawing the $b$ independent base words at time $t$, each rule $e$ has a state and a logit shift $z_{et}$ on a fixed output $j_e$. The state can be nonlinear, quantized or shared. It must depend only on information available before the current draws. Denote their class-frequency vector by $\hat q_t$ and put
$$
A_e=\sum_t [1_{\{y_t=j_e\}}z_{et}-q_{t,j_e}w_{et}],\quad
\hat A_e=\sum_t [1_{\{y_t=j_e\}}z_{et}-\hat q_{t,j_e}w_{et}],\quad
w_{et}=e^{z_{et}}-1,\quad V_e=\tfrac1{4b}\sum_t w_{et}^2.
$$
For each real $\lambda$, the conditional moment bound for a mean of $b$ independent Bernoulli variables makes $\exp(\lambda(\hat A_e-A_e)-\lambda^2 V_e/2)$ a nonnegative supermartingale as positions accumulate. Mix $\lambda$ against a centered normal with precision $\tau_e>0$ fixed before the draws. Completing the square gives a random variable of expectation at most one equal to $\sqrt{\tau_e/(\tau_e+V_e)}\exp((\hat A_e-A_e)^2/[2(\tau_e+V_e)])$. A union bound and Markov's inequality bound all errors by
$$
R(V;\tau,\alpha)=\sqrt{(V+\tau)[2\ln(1/\alpha)+\ln(1+V/\tau)]},\qquad \alpha=\delta/M .
$$
This is the two-sided normal-mixture boundary of Howard, Ramdas, McAuliffe and Sekhon. Its intrinsic time here is the predictable squared range of a rule applied to a base draw. [Primary paper, Eq. 14 and Proposition 5](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1810.08240v10)

For every post-selected packet $S$ with **distinct output classes**, simultaneously, its true normalized training gain along those realized histories is at least $\sum_{e\in S}(\hat A_e-R_e)$. Indeed its normalizer is $1+\sum_{e\in S}q_{t,j_e}w_{et}$ and $\ln(1+x)\le x$ for $x>-1$. Installation costs can be subtracted after this inequality. All rules may reuse the same purchased draws. Selected state trajectories have to be the same ones evaluated during the search. Exclusive outputs matter. With $q_j=0.1$, two shifts of one on the same realized class would give an additive surrogate $2-0.2(e-1)=1.6563$ even though the true gain is only $2-\ln(1+0.1(e^2-1))=1.5060$. A separate predictability failure occurs if a shift is chosen after a current draw misses its target. That selection can consistently hide its normalization cost. A new text or a fresh sample history requires its own assessment. [Sample-only fitter and physical packet](receipt_program.py)

**Exact variance of the ideal-state score.** For a single class on fixed text write $r_t=x_t-q_t$, $\eta_t=q_t-\hat q_t$, $v_t=q_t(1-q_t)/b$, and $k_{jt}=\rho(1-\rho)^{t-j-1}$ for $j<t$. Define $G=\sum_{j<t} k_{jt}r_jr_t$ and compute $\hat G$ using $\hat r=r+\eta$. If $h_t=\sum_{j<t} k_{jt}r_j+\sum_{u>t} k_{tu}r_u$, direct expansion gives
$$
\hat G-G=\sum_t h_t\eta_t+\sum_{j<t} k_{jt}\eta_j\eta_t,\qquad
\operatorname{Var}(\hat G\mid\text{tape})=\sum_t v_t h_t^2+\sum_{j<t} k_{jt}^2 v_jv_t .
$$
Distinct centered monomials are orthogonal by independence across times. The variance uses the unknown $q$ and gives an oracle diagnostic. For an observable interval, the same error is $\sum_t C_t\eta_t$ with predictable coefficient $C_t=\hat s_t+f_t$, where $\hat s_t=\sum_{j<t} k_{jt}\hat r_j$ and $f_t=\sum_{u>t} k_{tu} r_u$. Weighted Hoeffding bounds for the sampled reverse filter $\hat f_t$ give simultaneous radii $a_t=\sqrt{\sum_{u>t} k_{tu}^2\ln(2N/\delta_f)/(2b)}$ across $N$ queried filters, positions, classes and replications. Therefore $(4b)^{-1}\sum_t(|\hat s_t+\hat f_t|+a_t)^2$ upper bounds the predictable squared-range proxy on the filter event. The same receipts can be used in both calculations because the supermartingale uses the *true* predictable $C_t$ and the empirical upper bound enters only after intersecting events. Apply the monotone mixture radius above. Also bound $\sum s_t^2$ by $E^+=\sum(|\hat s_t|+a_t^{\rm past})^2$ with forward filter radii included in the union. The one-output ideal-state training gain at any selected $\gamma$ is at least $\gamma\hat G-|\gamma|R-\gamma^2 E^+/8$. Deterministic kernel-sum caps and the exact joint failure allocations are in the registered implementation. [Exact small-law enumeration](receipt_control.py) · [Gradient intervals, choices and oracle checks](receipt_selection.json)

Rejection sampling provides two other queries at a fixed state. With $M_t=\max_j e^{z_j}$, $Z_t=E_q e^z$, each accepted word costs $M_t/Z_t$ base proposals in expectation. Its class indicator gives an unbiased score gradient at that state. The wait $J$ for acceptance also yields the unbiased gain estimate $z(y)-\ln M_t+H_{J-1}$ since $E H_{J-1}=\sum_{k\ge1}(1-Z_t/M_t)^k/k$. Both use an unbounded possible number of proposals. The receipt certificate amortizes a fixed budget over all candidate rules. Rejection queries evaluate the selected tilted law directly and can use independent draws for the next memory update. The new replay reports the expected cost for its installed programs. [Inverse-binomial estimator, Eqs. 13–15](https://journals.plos.org/ploscompbiol/article?id=10.1371/journal.pcbi.1008483) · [New text replays](receipt_replay_result.json)

## How many memory states can improve prediction in every training environment?

**Exact state-count criterion for a finite one-step experiment.** A writer observes a cue $h\in H$, writes one of $K$ messages $b$, and loses access to the cue. It may draw private random bits. There are finitely many environments $e$ and outcome symbols $j\in J$. The deployed writer and reader use the same rules in every environment. A supplied positive base law $q_e(j\mid h)$ scores the outcome. The reader applies a fixed logit vector $z_b$ to that base and normalizes. The writer kernel $P(b\mid h)$ is conditionally independent of the future outcome given the cue and environment. Only $b$ passes from writer to reader. The loss is scored on the probability actually issued after receiving $b$. Define the signed residual array
$$
\mu_e(h,j)=\Pr_e(H=h,Y=j)-\Pr_e(H=h)\,q_e(j\mid h).
$$
Every row sums to zero. Set the logit at one reference output to zero, both for the messages and for the following candidate matrix $F$. Let $\mathcal S$ consist of all finite cue-by-output matrices satisfying $\langle\mu_e,F\rangle>0$ in every environment.

If $\mathcal S$ is empty, even carrying the entire cue cannot give a common strict improvement in this model. Equivalently the convex hull of the finitely many $\mu_e$ contains zero, by strict hyperplane separation. Otherwise the smallest number of private stochastic messages for strict improvement is
$$
K_{\rm random}=1+\min_{F\in\mathcal S}\dim\operatorname{aff}\{F(h,\cdot):h\in H\}.
$$
For a deterministic writer the exact minimum is $\min_{F\in\mathcal S}|\{F(h,\cdot):h\in H\}|$. Thus $K_{\rm random}\le\min(|H|,|J|)$ whenever there is a common improvement.

*Proof.* Given a working writer and reader, let $F(h,j)=\sum_b P(b\mid h)z_b(j)$. Jensen's inequality for each realized log normalizer gives gain at most $\langle\mu_e,F\rangle$. The rows of $F$ belong to the convex hull of the $K$ decoder vectors, and hence have affine dimension at most $K-1$. A deterministic writer uses at most $K$ distinct rows. Conversely any finite set of rows of affine dimension $r$ is contained in a simplex with $r+1$ vertices in that affine space. For example use coordinates in a containing box $[-L,L]^r$, then the simplex $x_i\ge-L$, $\sum_i(x_i+L)\le 2rL$. Barycentric coordinates supply the writer and the simplex vertices supply the logits. A deterministic writer may transmit the row index directly. For either construction put $a=\min_e\langle\mu_e,F\rangle>0$ and let $D$ be the largest within-message logit range. Scaling the decoder vectors by $\lambda$ gives worst gain at least $\lambda a-\lambda^2 D^2/8$ by Hoeffding's lemma. Taking $\lambda=4a/D^2$ proves strict improvement. Small rational perturbations preserve a strict finite margin, so a finite probability precision suffices for existence. Choosing a tiny margin can make attainable gain negligible and the required packet precision expensive. The state count alone prices neither. A fixed, externally prescribed writer restricts the possible factorizations to its own probability matrix.

The cardinality upper bound can be necessary. Put $d=\min(|H|-1,|J|-1)>0$ and $A_{ij}=F(h_i,j)-F(h_0,j)$ for $1\le i,j\le d$, with output 0 anchored. Require $A_{ii}>\sum_{j\ne i}\sigma_j A_{ij}$ for every sign pattern and row. The identity matrix passes. Every feasible matrix has $A_{ii}>\sum_{j\ne i}|A_{ij}|$, so it is nonsingular: in a hypothetical null vector take a component of maximal absolute value. Its row equation contradicts strict dominance. Each linear test is a feasible environment. Assign its coefficients to the indicated cells, balance every row at output 0 and scale the whole perturbation down around a uniform joint law. Cue marginals and the positive base stay uniform. The constructed family therefore needs $d+1$ stochastic messages. If either set is a singleton the trivial bound follows directly whenever improvement is possible.

**A private bit and an arbitrarily long deterministic ordering.** For $m\ge1$ the cues $h=0,\ldots,m$ are uniform, and the base predicts a fair bit. Environment $i=0,\ldots,m-1$ has conditional probability $1/2+\epsilon(1_{\{h=i+1\}}-1_{\{h=i\}})$ for outcome one, $0<\epsilon<1/2$. Cue names and source names can be permuted. Every environment has a fair marginal outcome. For a deterministic writer, common strict gain requires strictly increasing average logit from every cue to its successor. It needs exactly $m+1$ distinct states, by the criterion. One private stochastic bit suffices. More precisely its maximum worst-environment gain is
$$
d\!\left(\frac{2\epsilon}{m(m+1)}\right),\qquad
d(x)=\mathrm{kl}(\operatorname{Bern}(1/2+x)\,\|\,\operatorname{Bern}(1/2)).
$$
Up to swapping bit names, the only optimal encoder writes one with probability $\beta_h=h/m$. The optimal achieved gain thus decreases as $\Theta(\epsilon^2/m^4)$ for large $m$. The separation in *strict feasibility* must be read together with this rate.

*Proof of optimality over arbitrary two-probability readouts.* Order the two reported probabilities as $p_1\ge p_0$, with both interior. Write $w=E\beta_H$ and $c_i=\operatorname{Cov}_i(B,Y)=\epsilon(\beta_{i+1}-\beta_i)/(m+1)$. At fixed $w$ the gain is affine in $c_i$, with slope $\operatorname{logit}(p_1)-\operatorname{logit}(p_0)\ge0$. At zero covariance the bit and outcome are independent and the best gain is zero. Thus positive worst gain requires $s=\min_i(\beta_{i+1}-\beta_i)>0$. The proposed bit $B_*$ has $w_*=1/2$ and $c_*=\epsilon/[m(m+1)]$ in every environment. Its two conditional outcome probabilities are $1/2\pm 2c_*$. This gives the claimed gain.

For any competing bit, $is\le\beta_i\le 1-(m-i)s$, whence $ms\le2\min(w,1-w)$. In its worst environment its covariance is $c=ms\,c_*$. Starting from $B_*$, emit a fresh one with probability $w-ms/2$ on zero and $w+ms/2$ on one. These are valid probabilities, and they reproduce the competitor's $w$ and $c$. Those two numbers determine the binary joint law with fair $Y$. The best possible log gain from this degraded observation is at most that from $B_*$, by conditional entropy or strict concavity of binary entropy. Equality requires exact recovery of $B_*$ from the new bit because its two posteriors differ. With our orientation, $w=1/2$ and $ms=1$. The $m$ increments can then only be $1/m$. This argument compares the competitor in its worst environment; another environment may favor it. Randomized quantizer design also arises in distributed detection under other losses and information structures. [Dulek, Efendi and Varshney, 2024](https://doi.org/10.1109/TSP.2024.3439732)

**Physical price of the write.** A fixed allowance of $L$ fresh fair bits gives at most $2^L+1$ distinct writing probabilities, including zero and one. With one carried bit, common strict gain in the chain is possible exactly when $2^L\ge m$. Use any strictly increasing ladder on this grid and sufficiently small readout logits. Exact optimality at finite worst-case depth needs $m\mid 2^L$. A variable-depth sampler for a probability $p$ compares successive bits of a uniform random fraction to the binary expansion of $p$. If $2^k p$ is not an integer, every exact fair-bit sampler must leave at least one length-$k$ prefix undecided, since otherwise its probability has denominator dividing $2^k$. The comparison procedure attains that survival lower bound at every depth. Thus the least expected number is zero at the endpoints, $2(1-2^{-k})$ at a dyadic of reduced denominator $2^k$, and two at a non-dyadic. These prices assume fresh coins, a reset episode and a hidden stopping time. A clock, retained random seed, later cue access, batching or a side channel changes the resource model. A public schedule can implement time-sharing on repeated trials. Scoring an averaged forecast while the cue is still known also gives the reader more information.

Finite write precision also gives an exact loss curve. Put $N=2^L$, $r=m\lfloor N/m\rfloor/N$. If $r=0$ the optimum is zero. Otherwise with unconstrained readout precision it is $I(r/2,rc_*)$, where $I(w,c)=w d(c/w)+(1-w)d(c/(1-w))$. For any feasible minimum writing-probability increment $s>0$, the worst covariance is fixed and $w$ ranges inside $[ms/2,1-ms/2]$. At fixed $c$, $I(w,c)$ is symmetric about $1/2$ and convex in $w$ because each summand is a perspective of the convex function $d$. Its maximum over the interval occurs at an endpoint. The grid encoder $\beta_h=hs$ attains the lower endpoint with the same joint law in every environment, so one common Bayes readout suffices. These endpoint experiments are obtained from $B_*$ by retaining a one with probability $ms$ and otherwise emitting zero. The larger retention experiment can simulate any smaller one by additional thinning. The best attainable increment is therefore $\lfloor N/m\rfloor/N$. This prices the loss from a hard bound on private random bits even when that bound allows a strict gain.

**Ordinary-data acquisition.** For any finite collection of binary problems with a fair base, let $\nu_e(h)=E_e[1_{\{H=h\}}(Y-1/2)]$. A signed cue value $f_h\in[-1,1]$ specifies bit probability $(1+f_h)/2$. Its chance of agreeing with $Y$ in environment $e$ is exactly $1/2+\langle\nu_e,f\rangle$. With readouts symmetric about $1/2$, the optimal uniform gain is $d(a)$, where finite linear-programming duality gives
$$
a=\max_{\|f\|_\infty\le1}\min_e\langle\nu_e,f\rangle
 =\min_{\pi\in\Delta(E)}\left\|\sum_e\pi_e\nu_e\right\|_1 .
$$
Allowing asymmetric readouts can change the optimal value in general. The criterion $a>0$ is necessary and sufficient even for unrestricted readouts, by the state-count theorem. For $n_e$ independent ordinary cue/outcome pairs per identified training source, estimate each signed vector by its sample average. The $\ell_1$ error is a maximum over $2^{|H|}$ signs. Every signed summand lies in $[-1/2,1/2]$. With joint failure probability $\delta$ across $L$ fits, all source errors for any one fit are at most $\sqrt{(|H|\ln2+\ln(L|E|/\delta))/(2\min_e n_e)}$, by the one-sided Hoeffding bound and a union. Thus the smallest empirical correlation of the *rounded* writer minus this radius is a valid population margin no matter how the writer was chosen. Encode a conservative complementary readout using that margin. The measured implementation uses fixed precision and decodes its actual bytes. [Learning rule and exact garbling checks](robust_bit.py)

Training-source boundaries convey real information. For $m\ge2$, independently pool each chain pair over a uniform unobserved environment. The resulting law is identical to that of a family with $m-1$ entirely fair environments and one that raises the last cue and lowers the first by $\epsilon$. The adjacent perturbations telescope. In the second family no predictor can earn a strict gain in every environment. Unlimited untagged iid pairs cannot identify even the existence of the uniformly useful bit over these two families. The positive learning result uses observable training-file identity and supplies no environment label to the deployed reader. [Registered population and finite-sample evidence](EVIDENCE.md#learning-one-private-bit-that-helps-every-source)

### A streaming writer can need arbitrarily many states to transmit one useful bit

A static cue writer may evaluate the entire cue before writing its message. If the cue arrives one symbol at a time, the same message distribution must be updated by a common transition law. This changes the attainable value even for a binary report.

**Sharp unary state threshold.** Fix an integer $m\ge2$ and $0<\epsilon<1/2$. In environment $k\ge0$, the writer sees a run of identical input symbols of length $mk$ or $mk+1$ with equal probabilities. At a common final query the outcome is Bernoulli with mean $1/2-\epsilon$ or $1/2+\epsilon$, respectively. A reset distribution $\alpha$, one time-homogeneous $K$-state stochastic transition matrix $P$, and one binary reporting table are shared by all environments. Only the realized state is visible to the reader. Let $s$ be the least prime divisor of $m$. The maximum **infimum over all $k$** of the expected log gain relative to the fair base is zero for $K<s$, and is $d(\epsilon)$ for $K\ge s$.

A deterministic $s$-cycle distinguishes residues zero and one because $s$ divides $m$. It issues the two true probabilities. Knowing the run length is an upper bound on any reader's performance, proving optimality. Conversely, suppose $K<s$. Each closed irreducible class of $P$ contains a simple cycle of length $\ell\le K$ coprime to $m$. Between any two states of the class, take a path through this cycle and traverse the cycle enough times to make the path length a multiple of $m$. Thus $P^m$ is irreducible in each original closed class. Original transient states remain transient under sampling every $m$ transitions, since the probability of avoiding all closed classes tends to zero. Each closed class has a unique stationary distribution under $P^m$, namely its $P$-stationary one. All $P^m$-stationary distributions are therefore $P$-stationary.

For any finite reader logit column $z$, Jensen's upper tangent bounds the gain in environment $k$ by $(\epsilon/2)\alpha P^{mk}(P-I)z$. A limit point of $N^{-1}\sum_{k<N}\alpha P^{mk}$ is $P^m$-stationary by telescoping, and hence $P$-stationary. The average upper bound consequently tends to zero. A strictly positive uniform gain is impossible, and the fair readout attains zero. The interior target probabilities rule out any beneficial infinite logit on a reachable reporting event. The writer has no separate step counter, external clock or input-dependent timing channel. An offline writer already supplied the full length can send the answer with one deterministic bit.

The uniform quantifier matters even at two states. Start in a transient state which survives each symbol with probability $a\in(0,1)$ and otherwise enters an absorbing state. Predict $1/2-\epsilon(1-a)/(1+a)$ in the transient state and $1/2$ in the absorbing state. Conditional on surviving to the query, the relative likelihoods of the two lengths are $1:a$, independently of $k$. Consequently the exact gain is $a^{mk}(1+a)d(\epsilon(1-a)/(1+a))/2$. It is positive at every finite $k$ and has infimum zero. A finite range of successful length tests cannot establish a uniform computational capability. An exhaustive audit covers every deterministic transition map and reset through four states for $2\le m\le9$. The lower bound above also covers private stochastic updates. [Finite maps, resets and acquisition code](robust_bit.py) · [Verified values](robust_bit_result.json)

For a finite window there is also a uniform bound over a machine *chosen for that window*. If $K<s$, $S_m(P)=I+P+\cdots+P^{m-1}$ is invertible. A singular $S_m$ would give a nontrivial $m$th-root eigenvalue $\lambda$ of $P$. At a maximal-modulus coordinate of an eigenvector, equality in the triangle inequality forces all successors to have the same modulus and acquire phase $\lambda$ each step. Following successors to a simple cycle of length $\ell\le K$ gives $\lambda^\ell=1$, incompatible with $\gcd(m,\ell)=1$. Compactness gives finite $C_{m,K}=\max_P\|S_m(P)^{-1}\|_\infty$ for fixed $m,K$. For $a_N=N^{-1}\sum_{k<N}\alpha P^{mk}$, telescoping yields $\operatorname{TV}(a_N,a_NP)\le C_{m,K}/N$. With the two possible lengths pooled over the window, the Bayes reader from the terminal state gains at most $d(\epsilon)\operatorname{TV}(a_N,a_NP)$ by convexity of $d$, since $d(\epsilon x)\le |x|d(\epsilon)$ for $|x|\le1$. The same bound holds on the worst environment in any $N$ consecutive indices by changing the initial state law. This bound requires no a priori mixing-time limit.

For **two states and odd $m$**, the best worst-environment gain on $k=0,\ldots,N-1$ with $N\ge2$ has the sharper order $\Theta(\epsilon^2/n^2)$, $n=m(N-1)$. A two-state stochastic matrix has a second eigenvalue $\lambda\in[-1,1]$. At $\lambda<0$ the differences between the two terminal state laws alternate in sign across consecutive $k$ (row vectors of sum zero form a one-dimensional eigenspace). A fixed logit cannot pass both upper tangent tests. At $0\le\lambda<1$, $\alpha P^t=\lambda^t\alpha+(1-\lambda^t)\pi$ for a stationary $\pi$. Even revealing the latent choice of $\alpha$ versus $\pi$ in this mixture at $k=N-1$ bounds any terminal state's gain from above. Set $u=\lambda^n$ and $\Delta=u(1-\lambda)$. The two possible lengths have mixture-choice probabilities $u$ and $u\lambda$. Since $d(x)\le4x^2$ by the chi-square bound on KL, the gain is at most
$$
2\epsilon^2\left[\frac{\Delta^2}{u(1+\lambda)}+
                       \frac{\Delta^2}{2-u(1+\lambda)}\right]
 \le 2\epsilon^2\left[\frac4{(n+2)^2}+\frac1{n(n+1)}\right].
$$
The first term uses the maximum of $\lambda^n(1-\lambda)^2$. For the second use $1-\lambda^n\ge n\lambda^{n-1}(1-\lambda)$ and $\lambda^{n+1}(1-\lambda)\le1/(n+1)$. Zero-probability terms are interpreted by continuity. The identity kernel gives zero. For the matching lower bound take the transient construction with $a=n/(n+1)$. Its smallest gain occurs at the last index, $a^n\ge e^{-1}$ and $d(x)\ge2x^2$, giving at least $\epsilon^2/[e(2n+1)^2]$. Thus a learner allowed to retune a two-state machine for each finite horizon still loses quadratically with the largest tested length.

Positive transition noise can change the infinite-horizon optimum discontinuously. If every transition entry must be positive, $P^n$ converges to its stationary distribution and the uniform value is zero at every finite $K$. For an explicit noisy $s$-cycle, reset to the uniform law $\pi$ with probability $\eta>0$ per symbol and otherwise advance deterministically. Starting at zero produces $(1-\eta)^n\delta_{n\bmod s}+[1-(1-\eta)^n]\pi$. The phase signal decays even when the nominal state count reaches the arithmetic threshold. Observing an exact real-valued distribution over states would supply a different physical object from observing one realized state.

Gruska, Qiu and Zheng already give the exact **deterministic classification** threshold for lengths $kN$ and $kN+l$. Their Theorem 2 specializes at $l=1$ to the least prime divisor above. The additional question here gives the state a noisy binary forecasting task and evaluates worst-source expected log gain for stochastic updates, including finite windows and precision. Recent work also uses unary Markov-chain periodicity to analyze stochastic resolution of nondeterminism. [Gruska, Qiu and Zheng, §3, Theorem 2](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1404.1689v4) · [Resolving Nondeterminism by Chance, CONCUR 2025, §4](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/storage/00lipics/lipics-vol348-concur2025/html/LIPIcs.CONCUR.2025.32/LIPIcs.CONCUR.2025.32.html)

## Learning a reversible transition from short noisy report pairs

**PROVED.** Let a raw advance apply an unknown permutation $T$ to at most $N$ positions. Reset each episode independently and uniformly. A read returns $Z=\pm1$ with $\mathbb E[Z\mid s]=\rho f(s)$ for an unknown sign function $f$ and common $\rho>0$. Reads preserve the position and have independent noise given the path. Write $U f=f\circ T$ and use the uniform inner product. Two reads $l$ advances apart have product mean $C_l=\rho^2 c_l$, where $c_l=\langle f,U^l f\rangle$. In particular a fresh second read at lag zero measures the scale. If $\mu=\mathbb E Z$, the probability of a positive final report after observing $z$ is $(1+(\mu+z C_l)/(1+z\mu))/2$ for a first sign of positive probability. This specifies the ordinary conditional log-loss task.

The moments at lags **zero through $N-1$** determine every future pair forecast, given the marginal. Form $G_j=(c_{|i-h|})_{0\le i,h\le j}$, the Gram matrix of $f,\ldots,U^j f$. At the first singular $G_d$, the preceding Gram is positive definite. Solve $G_{d-1}a=(c_d,\ldots,c_1)^\top$. The projection has zero residual, so $U^d f=\sum_{i<d}a_iU^i f$. Applying $U^t$ gives the enduring update. If $G_{N-1}$ is nonsingular, these $N$ functions span the whole $N$-point space. A permutation with two or more cycles has a fixed eigenspace of dimension at least two, whereas the fixed-space projections of all $U^if$ are the same vector. Thus there is a single $N$-cycle and the update is $U^N f=f$. The horizon is sharp for $N\ge3$, even with known size and marginal. Put a single minus sign on an $N$-cycle, or on an $(N-1)$-cycle accompanied by a positive fixed point. Both give $c_l=1-4/N$ at every $1\le l<N-1$. At $N-1$ only the second returns to its starting observable.

The minimal polynomial $P=x^d-\sum a_i x^i$ has rational coefficients since the moments are rational. It divides the permutation's annihilator, the least common multiple of $x^m-1$ over its cycle lengths $m$. It is a product of distinct monic cyclotomic polynomials in $\mathbb Z[x]$. The least common multiple $r$ of their orders is the least $t>0$ with $U^t f=f$. This is also the least period of the correlations, since $c_t=1$ exactly at such returns. An integer streamer stores the first $d$ correlations as numerators over their common denominator $D$, which divides the number $n$ of positions. Every subsequent numerator has magnitude at most $D$. The coefficient of $x^i$ in $P$ has magnitude at most $\binom di$ by multiplying its unit-modulus roots. A signed accumulator of magnitude $D2^d$ suffices. The largest individual reduced denominator in an initial segment can be smaller than a future one. The divisibility statement concerns their least common multiple.

The period can reach the largest least common multiple of a partition of $n$, by marking one position per cycle. A deterministic machine producing the endpoint sequence on raw advances needs $r$ reachable states. Two different phases admit a continuation at which one returns and the other has at least two mismatched signs, hence $c_t\le1-4/n$. The same lower bound on state count holds for uniform correlation error strictly below $2/n$. This is a worst-continuation requirement. The integer recurrence, a period table and, for some fitted laws, a sum of divisibility indicators can all realize the same $r$ reachable configurations at different byte and time costs. Toeplitz moments and finite-dimensional linear dynamics have substantial antecedents. Here independent resets allow mixtures of cycles, and the uniform finite sign grid supplies a finite-sample certificate. [Korda, Putinar and Mezić](https://arxiv.org/abs/1710.06532) · [Arbabi and Mezić](https://arxiv.org/abs/1611.06664)

**PROVED, acquisition price.** If $n$ is known, $c_l=1-4k/n$ since equally many plus signs move to minus signs as the reverse. Distinct values are at least $4/n$ apart. For unknown $n\le N$ the union grid has spacing at least $4/N^2$. With $\rho\ge\rho_0>0$, estimating each $C_l$ and $C_0$ to $O(\rho_0^2/N^2)$ suffices to identify the required moments exactly. This follows by dividing simultaneous intervals with $C_0\ge\rho_0^2$ and using $|C_l|\le C_0$. Bounded-sign Hoeffding intervals give $O(\rho_0^{-4}N^4\log(N/\delta))$ independent pairs **per lag**, or $O(\rho_0^{-4}n^2\log(n/\delta))$ for known size. A data-dependent implementation can stop the Gram construction as soon as the needed exact moments are unambiguous. The common marginal and noise scale still incur estimation error in probability forecasts.

For a total-sample lower bound at unknown size, take balanced signs on $n=4s+2$ points. An involution exchanges $s$ opposite-sign pairs and fixes the rest. Compare it with the same construction at $n'=4s+6$ using $s+1$ exchanges. All even moments agree, and odd moments are $2/n$ and $2/n'$. The pair law has a uniform first sign and an independent product with success probability $(1+\rho^2 c_l)/2$. At every lag its KL between the two sources is $O(\rho^4/N^4)$ for $n,n'\asymp N$. Summing conditional KL over adaptive purchases and reducing the resulting transcript to the decision gives $\Omega(N^4\rho^{-4}\operatorname{kl}(1-\delta,\delta))$ expected pairs for exact recovery. At known $n$, changing one opposite-sign exchange near $n/4$ instead gives $\Omega(n^2\rho^{-4}\operatorname{kl}(1-\delta,\delta))$. These plants have the same period. A distinct pair gives a *period* lower bound in raw advances. On $n=2m$ points use a marked $m$-cycle or $(m-1)$-cycle, with one negative on the cycle. Fill the fixed points to make each source balanced. All lags below $m-1$ agree, and at every lag $|\Delta c_l|\le4/n$. For $\rho<1$, an informative pair costs at least $m-1$ advances and contributes at most $16\rho^4/[n^2(1-\rho^4)]$ KL. Thus period identification can cost $\Omega((1-\rho^4)n^3\rho^{-4}\operatorname{kl}(1-\delta,\delta))$ advances. Here $\operatorname{kl}$ is binary relative entropy. A ceiling on size matters for noisy exact identification. A balanced identity plant and an arbitrarily large balanced plant with one opposite-sign transposition approach the same law on every bounded number of paired probes, even at adaptively chosen lags. Truncating any almost surely finite stopping time on the identity plant then rules out uniform error below one half for exact period recovery without a capacity bound.

### From paired reports to forecasts after every observation

**PROVED.** Allow several permutation actions with known inverse spellings. Read a word $h$ left to right and write $U_{uv}=U_uU_v$, $v_h=U_{h^{-1}}f$. With $c(w)=\langle f,U_w f\rangle$, the pair experiment measures $\langle v_h,v_v\rangle=c(hv^{-1})$. After the first sign $z$ and word $h$, the posterior density relative to uniform is $(1+z\rho v_h)/(1+z\mu)$. The orbit vectors describe precisely the distinguishable deterministic word states for the paired readout. Equal vectors have identical continuations. If $v_h\ne v_k$, the suffix $h^{-1}$ gives respective correlations $1$ and $\langle v_k,v_h\rangle<1$. For a unit-cost inverse letter, a spanning set of $d$ orbit vectors can be learned with $O(|A|d^2)$ distinct exact moment probes of word length at most $2d$. Start at $f$, test successor vectors by their Gram projection residual and retain each independent vector. Once all basis successors lie in the span it is invariant under every action. Basis words have length at most $d-1$. Coordinates of every successor follow by solving the same Gram system. A new final observable uses its $d$ mixed moments against the basis for a paired forecast.

The full history requires conditioning on each report. Define $s\sim t$ if $f(T_w s)=f(T_w t)$ for every future word $w$. Its $q$ classes give the minimal deterministic distinctions from a **known starting class**. Algebraically, start at the constant function $1$ and close its linear span under the shifts $U_a$ and pointwise multiplication by $f$. This generates exactly all functions constant on the $q$ classes. Conjugating multiplication by shifts gives multiplication by every $U_w f$. The product of $(1\pm U_w f)/2$ over finitely many separating tests is a class indicator. In the other direction, both operations preserve class-constant functions. A report multiplies a prior density by $(1+z\rho f)/2$ before normalization. The linear span generated by shifts of $f$ alone need not contain these products.

An ordinary observation procedure acquires the quotient when reads are nondestructive and raw paths can be reversed. Start with tests $W=\{\epsilon\}$. From the *same* hidden reset position, read each $w\in W$ and $aw$ for each action $a$, returning after each test. Estimate a sign by independent votes. When equal $W$-signatures have unequal successor signatures, add a conflicting $aw$. On correct reads with every position covered in each round, every addition strictly refines the partition. At closure the sign is constant on each class and every action is well-defined on classes. Induction over words gives equality of every future sign, proving the partition is exactly $\sim$. A class cannot have been split by a test on which equivalent positions agree. Inverses make the inferred transitions permutations. For $n\le N$, $M$ uniform resets per round, $B$ votes per sign and amplitude at least $\rho_0$, the chance of a missing position or wrong construction sign is at most
$$
N^2 e^{-M/N}+ M(1+|A|)N^2 e^{-B\rho_0^2/2}.
$$
There are at most $N$ rounds, at most $(1+|A|)N$ words per round and each test pays twice its length in actions plus its reads. This consistency refinement is related to automata observation tables. Hidden uniform resets, a purchased return path and independent repeated signs supply the comparisons here. Persistent answer errors require a different argument. [Angluin, 1987](https://people.eecs.berkeley.edu/~dawnsong/teaching/s10/papers/angluin87.pdf) · [Local distinguishing experiments, IJCAI 1993](https://ijcai.org/proceedings/1993/150) · [Persistent-noise class queries, ICLR 2026](https://proceedings.iclr.cc/paper_files/paper/2026/hash/df01766dc88e5f1d475df4f7f3097d73-Abstract-Conference.html)

Given the graph, a belief over classes, the class reset probabilities and the report probabilities suffice for all old-task histories. Transitivity implies equal class sizes and therefore a uniform prior. In a general action graph the prior is the reset mass of each class. Under uniform latent reset, each old-task likelihood is constant inside the starting fiber and each action bijects fibers. The conditional distribution within a current fiber therefore stays uniform. A single new terminal report needs only its mean in each class. Earlier new-task reports can break that uniformity. Conditional on correct acquisition, fitted old noise and prior give cumulative expected excess over $H$ ordinary reports bounded by
$$
D(\pi\Vert\widehat\pi)+H\,\operatorname{kl}\!\left((1+\rho)/2,(1+\widehat\rho)/2\right).
$$
Use the joint law of the initial class, observed actions and reports, the chain rule, and then marginalize the class. A common history-dependent action policy contributes zero additional conditional KL. Exact transition identification alone supplies neither an unknown noise amplitude nor reset masses on disconnected components. Eight observable classes can still require a belief with many more than eight distinguishable values.

### The price of revisiting a hidden quantity

**PROVED.** At every reset draw $k$ hidden signs uniformly with product $\theta$, where one unknown $\theta\in\{-1,1\}$ is shared across resets. Each read of coordinate $i$ returns a fresh binary sign with conditional mean $\rho X_i$. In one pass, each coordinate may be read at most once in a reset. The law of the complete row is
$$
2^{-k}\left(1+\theta\rho^k\prod_{i=1}^k z_i\right).
$$
Expand the conditional likelihood over the uniform parity slice. Its only nonzero sign moments are the constant and the full parity. Every proper subset has exactly the same law in both worlds. In particular a cyclic readout of $k=3$ signs on four equiprobable positions in each world has equal marginals and equal pair correlations at *all* lags (even repeated-coordinate pairs agree), and opposite three-coordinate moments. Distinct ordinary prediction laws can survive complete endpoint acquisition.

For fixed advantage $\eta\in(0,1/2)$ over a fair guess, one-pass identification needs and suffices in order **$k\rho^{-2k}$ ordinary reads**. On completing a row its product gives one symmetric binary observation with amplitude $a=\rho^k$. Every preceding distinct-coordinate response is independent of the hypothesis. Conditional on their values, the last response multiplied by their product is a fresh channel use. This remains true when the learner interleaves, abandons or adaptively finishes rows. With equiprobable $\theta$, every completed row carries at most $C(a)=\ln2-h((1-a)/2)\le(\ln2)a^2$ nats, by the positive even-power series of $C$. A guess with advantage $\eta$ needs at least $C(2\eta)$ nats. Each completed row costs $k$ reads. Majority of $O_\eta(a^{-2})$ complete row products attains the bound. For prediction on that same one-pass source, even a learner that knows $\theta$ gains only $C(\rho^k)$ per complete row against independent fair signs. Exact structure acquisition and savings on a specified workload answer different questions. A mixture over the two given parity laws pays at most $\ln2$ cumulative regret relative to the true one, for every history.

If the learner can repeatedly read a persistent coordinate, the optimal expected read cost for fixed advantage and sufficiently large $k$ is instead
$$
\Theta_\eta\!\left(k+\frac{k\ln k}{\rho\ln((1+\rho)/(1-\rho))}\right),\qquad 0<\rho<1.
$$
For the upper bound, take independent majority votes for each coordinate and multiply the signs. A $b$-read vote errs with probability at most $(1-\rho^2)^{b/2}$. Since $-\frac12\ln(1-\rho^2)\ge \rho\ln((1+\rho)/(1-\rho))/4$, a union bound suffices. A cyclic scanner can take these votes in place with $k-1$ forward moves. The reads, uniform resets and the shared parity assumption are explicit physical inputs.

For the lower bound first give the learner one persistent vector and arbitrary coordinate addressing. Mix the two hypotheses equally so the initial coordinates are independent fair signs. For $\lambda=\ln((1+\rho)/(1-\rho))$, the posterior log odds are $L_i=\lambda\sum_{j\ {\rm reading}\ i} Z_j$. Given the adaptive transcript, coordinates remain independent since the policy and its stopping decisions contribute the same likelihood given past answers. Put $S=\sum_i|L_i|$. The absolute posterior parity bias $V=\prod_i|\tanh(L_i/2)|$ satisfies $V\le\exp(-k e^{-S/k})$ by $1-|\tanh(x/2)|\ge e^{-|x|}$ and convexity of the exponential. Advantage $\eta$ implies $\mathbb E V\ge2\eta$ and $\Pr(V\ge\eta)\ge\eta/(1-\eta)$. On this event $S\ge k\ln(k/\ln(1/\eta))$. Predictable sampling gives $\mathbb E\sum_i X_i L_i=\rho\lambda\,\mathbb E B$ for total reads $B$ of finite expectation, by summing the conditional expectation of each purchased read. Also $\mathbb E[X_iL_i\mid\text{transcript}]=|L_i|\tanh(|L_i|/2)\ge |L_i|-1$. This yields the logarithmic term. A single unread bit forces $V=0$, so $\mathbb E B\ge2\eta k$. The lower bound extends to arbitrary fresh resets sharing $\theta$. From one persistent vector a simulator can privately multiply it by a fresh uniform even-parity mask on each reset, and multiply each observed sign by its mask coordinate. Given the persistent vector, the simulated rows are independent uniform draws from its parity slice. Granting free masks and addressing only strengthens the lower bound on purchased reads. Noisy decision-tree parity and the posterior-product method have established antecedents. [Feige, Raghavan, Peleg and Upfal](https://doi.org/10.1137/S0097539791195877) · [Evans and Pippenger, Theorem 5.1](https://www.cs.ubc.ca/~will/papers/dectree.pdf#page=10)

For a fixed intermediate exposure schedule $m_i$ per coordinate, the same calculation quantifies how much ordinary experience is worth. Let $Q$ mix $\theta$ equally and let $b_i=\mathbb E_Q[X_i\mid\text{its }m_i\text{ reads}]$. The two row densities relative to $Q$ are $1\pm\prod_i b_i$. Consequently the information about $\theta$ lies between $\frac12\prod_i A_{m_i}$ and $(\ln2)\prod_i A_{m_i}$ nats, with $A_m=\mathbb E_Q b_i^2$, by independence and $t^2/2\le C(t)\le(\ln2)t^2$. A missing coordinate has $A_0=0$, a single read has $A_1=\rho^2$, and two reads give $A_2=2\rho^2/(1+\rho^2)$. The inequality also applies conditionally on an exogenous random schedule. Adaptive repeat decisions can correlate the factors, so the deterministic product of their individual expectations is specific to independent exposure.

## Discovering which earlier observations to combine

**PROVED.** Let a row start with $d-1$ independent fair hidden signs. Its final hidden sign is $\theta\prod_{i\in S}X_i$, with unknown $S$ of size $s-1$ and unknown $\theta=\pm1$. Every raw report has independent symmetric noise of known amplitude $\rho\in(0,1)$. Put $A=S\cup\{d\}$, $M=\binom{d-1}{s-1}$ and $C(a)=\ln2-h((1+a)/2)$ in nats. With one report per coordinate, the observable row density relative to $d$ fair independent signs is $1+\theta\rho^s\chi_A(z)$, where $\chi_A(z)=\prod_{i\in A}z_i$. Expanding the binary likelihood kills every other nonconstant character. Thus the observed inputs are themselves uniform, and the observed target is their affine parity with independent effective label-noise amplitude $\rho^s$. At known size this is a precise reduction to ordinary noisy parity. A reduction whose cost depends polynomially on inverse noise gap can still cost exponentially in $s$. [Bshouty and Haddad, RANDOM 2024](https://drops.dagstuhl.de/entities/document/10.4230/LIPIcs.APPROX/RANDOM.2024.38) · [Full reading and scope](../../literature/notes/BSHOUTY-HADDAD-2024-NOISY-PARITY-SPARSITY.md)

Each row contributes information only when the learner reads its **last still-unread true support coordinate**, including the target. Before that purchase all sampled signs in the row are fair. At that purchase the conditional sign bias is $\pm\rho^s$, and every subsequent coordinate outside the support is fair. Relative to the fair law, the transcript therefore has KL exactly $C(\rho^s)\mathbb E_A N_A$, where $N_A$ counts rows in which all coordinates in $A$ were read. This chain-rule identity permits adaptively chosen orders, partial rows, interleaving and stopping with finite expected purchases. Average over a uniform unknown support at a fixed offset. Mutual information is bounded above by that average KL to the same reference law. Fano's inequality then gives
$$
\mathbb E N_A\ \ge\ \frac{\ln M-h(\delta)-\delta\ln(M-1)}{C(\rho^s)}
$$
for support error at most $\delta$, $M\ge2$. Each covered row costs at least $s$ ordinary reads. A learner taking complete rows pays $d$ each. Reading each candidate subset in separate rows can avoid ever completing a $d$-coordinate row when $s<d$. Exhaustive correlations on complete rows match the $\rho^{-2s}\log(M/\delta)$ sample order at fixed known $s$: only the true character has nonzero mean, and a union bound on the bounded signed products separates it. This is an information statement and permits exponential hypothesis-search work.

Repeated reads change which learning operation is feasible. Take $L$ fresh rows and an odd number $b$ of independent reads of each coordinate. Decode the majority at each coordinate, then solve the binary linear equations for the unknown mask and intercept. Each observed input row eliminates the affine hypotheses that predict the wrong decoded target. The implementation stores a basis of these constraints and constructs a mask by back substitution. The failure probability is at most
$$
dL(1-\rho^2)^{b/2}+(2^d-2)2^{-L}.
$$
The first term union-bounds imperfect majority votes. The second bounds rank deficiency on the true uniform rows: each nonconstant affine function is zero on all $L$ inputs with probability $2^{-L}$, and the constant one never vanishes. Thus $L=d+O(\log(1/\delta))$ rows suffice with $b=O(1+\log(dL/\delta)/[-\ln(1-\rho^2)])$. A packed elimination basis uses $d(d+1)$ bits up to alignment and $O(Ld)$ row XORs of width $d+1$. If the promised sparsity is available, enumerating $2M$ affine candidates can reduce the sufficient clean-row count to $\lceil\log_2(2M/\delta)\rceil$. The registered learner received the affine family and the coordinate order. The subset, intercept and coefficients of a particular update came from ordinary observations.

An installed model incurs a separate physical cost. With $m$ observed input reads per coordinate and one unseen noisy target, its conditional signed prediction is $\rho\theta\prod_{i\in S}b_i$, where $b_i$ is that coordinate's posterior mean. For $m=1$ the advantage over a fair terminal forecast is exactly $C(\rho^s)$. Consider repeated unmarked one-pass rows with a known initial reset, a nonempty mask and zero-based earliest selected input $h$. The exact deterministic streaming predictor needs **$2d-h-1$ reachable states**. Phase $0$ through $h$ has one reachable running parity each, and all later phases have two. Different phases can be continued until exactly one forecasts the terminal report, whose probability differs from $1/2$ by $\rho^s/2$. Different parities in the same phase admit an identical continuation to opposite terminal forecasts. All continuations have positive probability. The lower bound survives uniform absolute probability error strictly below $\rho^s/4$. A supplied coordinate label or a larger admissible error changes the count. For distinct supports on a common equal-depth workload, the signed conditional means are orthogonal and have squared norm $\rho^2 A_m^{s-1}$ with $A_m=\mathbb E b_i^2$. A collision of two installed packets must cost at least $\rho^2 A_m^{s-1}/4$ nats on one task, since squared distance between their mean predictions is at least $2\rho^2 A_m^{s-1}$ and signed-mean KL is at least half squared error. A fixed-width installation beating this threshold uniformly needs at least $\lceil\log_2(2M)\rceil$ bits. A Bayesian mixture over a supplied dictionary of all $2M$ models has cumulative log regret at most $\ln(2M)$, with a separate cost to represent or evaluate that mixture.

### What an observation budget reveals about an arbitrary prediction rule

**PROVED.** Let $X$ have independent fair binary coordinates. A terminal sign has conditional mean $\kappa f(X)$ with $|f|\le1$ and $0<\kappa<1$. Its noise is conditionally independent of the earlier observations given $X$. A schedule $m=(m_i)$ chosen before the data determines the number of independent symmetric reads of each coordinate, and is known to the forecaster. It may itself be random. Write $Kf=\mathbb E[f(X)\mid\text{observations},m]$ and expand $f=\sum_S f_S\chi_S$ in the uniform Walsh basis. The likelihood factors by coordinate, so $K\chi_S=\phi_S=\prod_{i\in S} b_i$. Conditional on $m$, distinct $\phi_S$ are orthogonal because a coordinate appearing exactly once has mean zero. Therefore
$$
\|K(f-g)\|_2^2=\sum_S w(S)(f_S-g_S)^2,\qquad
w(S)=\mathbb E_m\prod_{i\in S}A_{m_i}.
$$
The expectation of a product matters when a schedule couples the purchases. A source can expose each individual input frequently and never expose a particular interaction. For the single-pass binary symmetric channel this recovers squared Walsh attenuation $\rho^{2|S|}$. Conditional-expectation spectra and their relation to bit estimation are established tools. [Calmon et al., principal inertia components](https://arxiv.org/abs/1704.00820) · [O'Donnell, Boolean noise operators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2105.10386)

For $|g|\le1$, conditional log-loss excess $\mathcal D_E(f,g)$ under exposure $E$ lies between $\kappa^2\|K_E(f-g)\|^2/2$ and $\kappa^2\|K_E(f-g)\|^2/[2(1-\kappa^2)]$. Signed Bernoulli KL is the Bregman divergence of $C$ and $C''(u)=1/(1-u^2)$. Hence the sharp multiplicative transfer factor for **squared prediction error** is $\max_S w_D(S)/w_T(S)$ from training interface $T$ to deployment $D$, omitting joint zero modes. Positive deployment weight on a training zero makes uniform transfer impossible. For log loss the same ratio is necessary in the limit of small single-character perturbations and, divided by $1-\kappa^2$, sufficient globally. Identical quadratic weights can have distinct log gains. Train with one read of a bit of reliability $r$. In deployment take two reads with probability $(1+r^2)/2$ and zero otherwise. The second moment remains $r^2$, but the nonzero posterior magnitude $2r/(1+r^2)$ is larger. The ratio $C(x)/x^2$ strictly increases for $x>0$, so this deployment has strictly larger gain for $f(X)=X$ against a fair forecast.

There is an exact global KL criterion even for channels whose spectra have different bases. Fix the same latent prior, let $K_E$ be conditional expectation for any known observation channel independent of the task given $X$, and use its observation-law adjoint. The inequality $\mathcal D_D(f,g)\le c\,\mathcal D_T(f,g)$ for *all* bounded $f,g$ holds exactly when, at every interior task $u$,
$$
K_D^*\,\operatorname{diag}\!\left(\frac1{1-\kappa^2(K_Du)^2}\right)K_D
\ \preceq\ c\,K_T^*\,\operatorname{diag}\!\left(\frac1{1-\kappa^2(K_Tu)^2}\right)K_T .
$$
Indeed the excess is the Bregman divergence of $\Phi_E(f)=\mathbb E\,C(\kappa K_E f)$. The desired inequality is equivalent to convexity of $c\Phi_T-\Phi_D$. Its Hessian is the displayed difference times $\kappa^2$. Boundary cases follow by continuity. At $u=0$ this gives ordinary Gram order. Adaptive access may create off-diagonal terms even for independent fair bits. If one sees $X_1$ and sees $X_2$ only when $X_1=+1$, then $K X_2=K(X_1X_2)$. Both nonzero directions are visible but their difference is invisible. Classical less-noisy channel order uses different quantifiers over input distributions. [Makur and Polyanskiy](https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.06877)

There is an ordinary-data learning law for a specified collection $\mathcal F$ of interactions. From $L$ independent training rows with terminal sign $Y$, estimate $f_S$ by the sample mean of $Y\phi_S/(\kappa w_T(S))$ wherever $w_T(S)>0$. Orthogonality gives expectation $f_S$ and variance at most $1/(L\kappa^2 w_T(S))$. At deployment release $\kappa\sum_{S\in\mathcal F}\widehat f_S\phi_S$, clipped to $[-\kappa,\kappa]$. Its expected excess is at most
$$
\frac{\kappa^2\sum_{S\notin\mathcal F}w_D(S)f_S^2+
L^{-1}\sum_{S\in\mathcal F}w_D(S)/w_T(S)}
{2(1-\kappa^2)} .
$$
Clipping can only reduce squared error to the true signed mean. In particular, growing a feature set has estimation cost determined by the *relative* observation weights. This bound is for a fixed collection, or one chosen on an independent split. It assumes the conditional-expectation features and training weights are known. For an unknown pure parity of degree $k$, every lower-degree residual correlation is exactly zero. The binary elimination learner gives a way to construct this one class of high-degree features without following such correlations or enumerating masks. The guarantee for arbitrary $f$ still prices the candidate features.

### How to allocate a fixed number of reads before the task is known

**PROVED for identical binary symmetric read channels and a uniform prior.** Suppose the eventual query may involve any fixed $k$ of $n$ coordinates, $1\le k\le n$. The query subset is fixed before the policy's private randomization and revealed to the forecaster after the purchases, before it reports its probability. The objective here is the worst quadratic visibility $w(S)$ over such subsets. An episode permits at most $B$ reads. Among schedules independent of the read outcomes, the exact optimum is
$$
\frac{[t^k](1+A_jt)^{n-e}(1+A_{j+1}t)^e}{\binom nk},
\qquad j=\lfloor B/n\rfloor,\quad e=B-nj .
$$
Randomly permute the balanced allocation to attain this value on every fixed query. To see optimality, average over the $\binom nk$ subsets. For any count vector this is the elementary symmetric polynomial $e_k(A_{m_1},\dots,A_{m_n})/\binom nk$. The sequence $A_m$ is increasing by posterior martingale variance and strictly concave at every finite $m$ for $0<\rho<1$, as proved below. Moving a read from a count at least two larger to the smaller increases the pair sum. The pair product also increases because the marginal increment at the smaller count is larger and the other factor is larger. Expanding $e_k$ in the pair sum and product shows this move never reduces visibility. For $B\le n$ the answer simplifies to $\rho^{2k}\binom Bk/\binom nk$.

Here is the concavity step. Put $\alpha=\operatorname{atanh}\rho$, $c=\operatorname{sech}\alpha$, and $q=e^{-\alpha}$. For $S_m$ a sum of $m$ independent fair signs, writing the marginal likelihood relative to fair draws gives $1-A_m=c^m\mathbb E\operatorname{sech}(\alpha S_m)$. Define $F(z)=(1+z)/(1-z)$ and the product
$$
P(z)=\prod_{l\ge1}F(q^{2l-1}z)F(q^{2l-1}/z).
$$
It converges locally uniformly away from its poles and zero or infinity. It is positive on the unit circle and $P(q^2z)=-P(z)$ by cancellation. For its Laurent coefficient $p_h$, shrinking the unit contour to radius $q^2$ crosses only the simple pole at $q$. The residue identity $(1+q^{-2h})p_h=q^{-h-1}\operatorname{Res}(P,q)$ gives $p_h/p_0=\operatorname{sech}(\alpha h)$. Thus $1-A_m$ is the $m$th moment of $c\cos t$ under the density $P(e^{it})/(2\pi p_0)$. Its second difference integrates $(c\cos t)^m(1-c\cos t)^2$. For even $m$ this integral is positive. For odd $m$, pair $z=e^{it}$ and $-z$, with $x=\cos t>0$. Since $P(-z)=1/P(z)$ and
$$
P(z)=\prod_{l\ge1}
\frac{1+x\,\operatorname{sech}((2l-1)\alpha)}
     {1-x\,\operatorname{sech}((2l-1)\alpha)}
>\frac{1+cx}{1-cx},
$$
the paired contribution is positive as well. This proves strict quadratic diminishing returns for a fair binary latent sign. The Fourier coefficients of this positive kernel are also those of a normalized Jacobi elliptic function. Biased priors can violate the concavity, as Bikhchandani and Mamer's Bernoulli example demonstrates. [NIST DLMF, Jacobi Fourier series](https://dlmf.nist.gov/22.11#E3) · [Bikhchandani and Mamer, Proposition 1 and Example 5](https://escholarship.org/content/qt8dz8t6c2/qt8dz8t6c2.pdf)

**PROVED for actual terminal log gain at every hard cap.** Put $a=\operatorname{atanh}\rho$ and $B_{m,l}=\mathbb E b_m^{2l}$. The same randomly permuted balanced allocation maximizes the worst expected log gain over a fair terminal forecast. Its value is
$$
\sum_{l\ge1}\frac{\kappa^{2l}}{2l(2l-1)\binom nk}
[t^k](1+B_{j,l}t)^{n-e}(1+B_{j+1,l}t)^e .
$$
In particular $B\le n$ gives $\binom Bk C(\kappa\rho^k)/\binom nk$. This conclusion needs more than concavity of the squared posterior mean. For instance $B_{2,2}=8\rho^4/(1+\rho^2)^3$ exceeds $2B_{1,2}$ when $\rho=1/2$.

The scalar step is that $G_m(t)=\mathbb E C(t b_m)$ is concave in integer $m$ for every $0\le t\le1$. Here are details for $0<t<1$. Write $c=\operatorname{sech}a$, $\beta=\operatorname{atanh}t$, let $L$ average the two shifts by $\pm a$ and put $D=I-cL$. The residual is $C(t)-G_m(t)=c^m (L^m h)(0)$ with $h(x)=\cosh x[C(t)-C(t\tanh x)]$. Write $g(x)=\operatorname{sech}x$. Then $h=K*g$ where $K(x)=\sinh(\beta-|x|)/(2\cosh\beta)$ for $|x|\le\beta$ and vanishes outside. In fact $(1-\partial_x^2)h=g(x)-\operatorname{sech}(\beta)[g(x-\beta)+g(x+\beta)]/2$ by differentiation. The jumps of $K'$ give the same equation for $K*g$, and both solutions decay at both infinities.

First, $D^2g$ has one change of sign from positive to negative on the positive half-line. Indeed $H=Dg=\sinh^2(a)g(x-a)g(x)g(x+a)>0$, and
$$
\frac{cLH}{H}=\operatorname{sech}(2a)
\frac{1+\tanh(a)\tanh(2a)\tanh^2x}{1-\tanh^2(2a)\tanh^2x}
$$
increases from below one to above it. The folded kernel $K(x-y)+K(x+y)$ on $x,y\ge0$ preserves that order of signs under integration. To verify this, on $x+y\le\beta$ it is proportional to $2\sinh(\beta-\max(x,y))\cosh(\min(x,y))$. Elsewhere it is proportional to $\sinh(\beta-|x-y|)$ on its support. The $x$ derivative of its log is nondecreasing in $y$ on each piece. Crossing $y=x$ gives an upward jump. At $y=\beta-x$, it jumps from $-\coth(\beta-x)$ to $-\coth(2(\beta-x))$ if $x>\beta/2$, and from $\tanh x$ to $\coth(2x)$ if $x<\beta/2$. The endpoints of the support move right as $x$ increases. Thus for $x_2>x_1$ the second kernel row divided by the first is nondecreasing in $y$ on their common support, with the missing left and right intervals ordered consistently. Multiplying a positive-then-negative integrand by this ratio cannot turn a nonpositive integral positive. Since $D$ commutes with convolution, $D^2h=K*D^2g$ has at most one change from positive to negative.

Second, the even and odd lattice sums of $D^2h$ are nonnegative. Let $E(v)=\sum_{j\in\mathbb Z}g(v+2ja)$ and $O(v)=E(v+a)$. The Fourier coefficients of $E(v)$ are proportional to $\operatorname{sech}(\pi^2 j/(2a))$, by integrating the periodization and using $\int g(x)e^{-iux}\,dx=\pi\operatorname{sech}(\pi u/2)$ over the line.
