The latter identity follows by shifting the contour by $i\pi$. The Jacobi product proved above for these coefficients is increasing in $\cos(\pi v/a)$. Therefore $E$ is maximal at zero and minimal at $a$. For the earlier product $P$ with $q=e^{-a}$, summing its even and odd Fourier coefficients gives
$$
\min\{E(v)/O(v),O(v)/E(v)\}
\ge \frac{O(0)}{E(0)}
=\frac{P(1)-P(-1)}{P(1)+P(-1)}\ge\frac{2c}{1+c^2}.
$$
The last inequality follows from $P(-1)=1/P(1)$ and the first factor $P(1)\ge(1+c)/(1-c)$. Integration against the nonnegative $K$ extends both ratio inequalities to the even and odd lattice sums $E_h,O_h$ of $h$. The two totals of $D^2h$ are $(1+c^2)E_h-2cO_h$ and $(1+c^2)O_h-2cE_h$. On each parity lattice every centered partial sum of $D^2h$ is also nonnegative by the sign-change result. Binomial random-walk weights decrease with absolute distance from zero on their parity lattice, so summation by parts gives $(L^mD^2h)(0)\ge0$. This is precisely the nonnegative second difference of the residual, after multiplying by $c^m$. Continuity gives the endpoints in $t$.

Finally, average the log gain over $k$-subsets. It is symmetric in the read counts and separately concave by conditioning on the other coordinate votes and applying $G_m(t)$. Its marginal gain from an extra read in one coordinate is nondecreasing in any other coordinate's count. To see this, expand $C$ in positive even powers, and use that every $B_{m,l}$ is increasing by conditional Jensen applied to the posterior martingale. Each term is a positive multiple of a product of these moments. For counts $u<v-1$, the marginal of adding at $u$ with other count $v-1$ is at least the marginal at $v-1$ with other count $v-1$ by separate concavity. By symmetry and the increasing marginal property it is at least the cost of removing the final vote at $v$ with other count $u$. This proves balancing and the displayed value. The proof uses a fair initial sign, identical conditionally independent channels, and allocations committed before the votes.

A budget of $B$ **in expectation** has a different optimum. Let $J_m$ be the expected gain from giving every coordinate the same depth $m$. For squared visibility $J_m=A_m^k$. For actual terminal log gain against a fair forecast, $J_m=\mathbb E C(\kappa\prod_{i=1}^k b_{i,m})$ for independent coordinate votes. In either case the optimal worst-query value among exogenous schedules is the upper concave envelope of $m\mapsto J_m$ at $B/n$. For the quadratic case, AM–GM bounds $\prod_{i\in S} A_{m_i}$ by $\sum_{i\in S}A_{m_i}^k/k$. For log gain expand $C(x)=\sum_{l\ge1}x^{2l}/[2l(2l-1)]$ and apply the same argument separately to the nonnegative moments $\mathbb E b_{i,m_i}^{2l}$. Averaging over subsets gives a bound by the equal-depth payoff at the depth of a uniformly sampled coordinate. Conversely, give all coordinates the same random depth supported on the two integers defining an upper chord. This matches the bound and equalizes the tasks. Existence follows from bounded increasing $J_m$ and tightness of integer laws with bounded mean, or by truncation and passage to a limit. At $\rho=1/2$, $k=2$ and mean depth one, equal chances of common depths zero and two yield quadratic visibility $2/25$ versus $1/16$ at fixed depth one.

Observed feedback can improve on every exogenous schedule with the same hard cap. For two fair hidden bits and four reads, query the first bit twice and the second once. On disagreement of the first two observations, repair the first bit with the last read. Otherwise read the second. This attains $A_2^2+(1-\rho^2)\rho^4/2$ for the two-bit parity, strictly above the balanced optimum $A_2^2$. The final allocation is independent of the true signs but depends on the observed noise. Independence of the count schedule from latent signs alone does not justify the factorized exogenous formula. For a general finite hard cap, a finite Bellman recursion gives the exact adaptive optimum for a fixed unknown $k$-subset. Symmetrizing the policy in the coordinate names equates its worst and mean task gain. The sufficient state for planning is the sorted vector of absolute signed vote counts. At a coordinate of magnitude $j$ the next magnitude is $j+1$ with probability $(1+\rho\tanh(\alpha j))/2$ and $|j-1|$ otherwise. Terminal value is the mean over subsets of the product of their squared posterior means, or of $C(\kappa\prod b_i)$ for log gain. Backward induction maximizes over the next coordinate. Physical execution must still identify and reach that coordinate and retain the signs required by the eventual query.

The homogeneous read reliability for this design can itself be acquired without latent labels. Buy two independent reports of the same fair hidden sign per calibration episode. Their product has mean $\rho^2$. Given a known positive lower bound $\rho_0$, project its empirical mean onto $[\rho_0^2,1]$ and take the square root. A product-mean error $\epsilon$ gives $|\widehat\rho-\rho|\le\epsilon/(2\rho_0)$. For any policy with $B$ purchases and any terminal forecast clipped to $[(1-\kappa)/2,(1+\kappa)/2]$, couple the hidden vector, target, policy randomness and successive reads under the two channel parameters. Until a read differs the policy takes the same actions. The discrepancy probability is at most $B|\widehat\rho-\rho|/2$, so expected log loss differs by at most this times $\ln((1+\kappa)/(1-\kappa))$, uniformly in the policy and fixed query. Optimizing the policy and predictions under the fitted law therefore costs at most $B|\widehat\rho-\rho|\ln((1+\kappa)/(1-\kappa))$ additional worst-query loss under the true law. This trains a policy within the declared symmetric channel model. Learning an unknown observation channel from unmatched passive histories is a separate identification problem.

## What ordinary paired observations identify across an unfamiliar view

A source has a common reset law $\mu$ on hidden states. On a reset an independently emitted categorical old report $T$, new report $D$ and outcome $Y$ have conditional distributions $\pi_A(\cdot\mid h)$ for $A=T,D,Y$. Training sees selected pairs, including independent same-view twins when purchased. Deployment sees an ordinary $D$ and predicts a full distribution on $Y$. The unknown population joint block $G_{DY}$ determines that prediction on every supported $D$. Different reset distributions across the panels, shared emission noise or unrecorded selection on a report change the observation equations.

**PROVED, exact identification.** Stack the three conditional vectors into $v_h$ and let $e_A$ indicate the entire coordinate block for view $A$. The possible missing block is exactly the projection of the completely positive matrices
$$
G=\sum_i w_i w_i^\top,\quad w_i\ge0,\quad
G(e_T-e_D)=G(e_T-e_Y)=0,\qquad e_T^\top G e_T=1
$$
that agree with the measured joint tables. Indeed the source provides $w_h=\sqrt{\mu_h}\,v_h$. Conversely both block-sum differences have squared norm zero in the factorization. Thus each nonzero $w_i$ has the same strictly positive sum $s_i$ in every block. Dividing it by $s_i$ gives three valid emission distributions, and $\sum_i s_i^2=1$ supplies the common prior. The least attainable CP-rank is the smallest possible hidden cardinality for arbitrary reset weights. A ceiling of $K$ restricts the search to completions of CP-rank at most $K$. A fixed uniform reset over $K$ phases additionally requires $K$ factors with all $s_i=K^{-1/2}$. For instance a deterministic binary report with perfect twins and mass $1/3$ on one symbol needs at least three uniform phases, while two nonuniform phases suffice. Finite view alphabets also give a finite representation for an arbitrary reset space by taking the convex hull of the compact set of outer products. This characterization is about feasibility and does not establish an efficient general solver. Conditional-iid mixture factorizations and proxy rank conditions have precedents in other inference problems. [Stein, Ozdaglar and Parrilo](https://arxiv.org/abs/1307.3586) · [Miao, Geng and Tchetgen Tchetgen](https://arxiv.org/abs/1609.08816)

Each event probability $\pi_A(i\mid h)$ is a vector in $L^2(\mu)$. Project the $D$ and $Y$ vectors onto the span of the $T$ vectors. The inner products of their projections form $G_{DT}G_{TT'}^\dagger G_{TY}$, where $TT'$ denotes independent old twins. Their orthogonal residual cross-products give exactly the missing correction. Zero residual in either view or mutually orthogonal residuals suffices for the projected law. With measured self twins the corresponding diagonal residual blocks are observable Schur complements, and Cauchy–Schwarz bounds each correction. In particular if the measured old twin block has rank $K$ and every allowed source has at most $K$ positive atoms, its old vectors span the whole latent space. One low-rank fit certifies its own projection only. Identification requires all compatible laws to agree.

**PROVED, positivity and capacity give distinct information.** Let all three reports be fair signs, their independent self twins agree surely, and $c_{TD}=c_{TY}=-1/2$. Perfect twin agreement forces deterministic emissions at each latent atom. Put $U=TD,V=TY$ and $r=\mathbb E DY$. The joint masses of $(U,V)$ in order $++,+-,-+,--$ must be $(r,1-r,1-r,2+r)/4$. Nonnegativity gives $0\le r\le1$, and taking an independent fair $T$ realizes every point. The sign Gram determinant is $(1-r)(r+1/2)$, so positive semidefiniteness and nonnegative pair tables instead allow $-1/2\le r\le1$. The one-hot Gram is obtained from these signs and the constant by a congruence and is entrywise nonnegative throughout that interval.

The exact minimum number of nonuniform latent atoms at $r=0$, $0<r<1/2$, $r=1/2$, $1/2<r<1$ and $r=1$ is respectively $6,7,5,6,4$. For a group $(u,v)$ of mass $w_{uv}$ let $b_{uv}$ be the difference of its two $T$ sign masses. Fair marginal constraints impose $\sum b=\sum ub=\sum vb=0$, hence $b_{uv}=zuv$ with $|z|\le\min w$. A positive group contributes two atoms unless $|z|=w$, when it contributes one. Taking the largest allowed $|z|$ and counting groups gives the stated minima, including the zero-mass endpoints. A four-state promise identifies $D=Y$ at every reset despite two nonzero projection residuals. With a five-state promise the exact set $\{1/2,1\}$ is disconnected.

For unrestricted cardinality, convexity of conditional log regret and sign-flip symmetry permit a single forecast $q=\Pr(Y=D\mid D)$. Its worst case over the physical class is the larger Bernoulli KL at $p=1/2$ and $1$. Equating them gives $q=4/5$, with worst regret $\ln(5/4)$. A least-favourable prior assigns probability $3/5$ to $p=1$ and $2/5$ to $p=1/2$. This prior concerns source uncertainty, distinct from a source's reset weights. The projected law gives $q=5/8$ and worst regret $\ln(8/5)$. The independent endpoint implies that maximized worst-case **gain over fair** is zero, attained by fair prediction. Minimax excess relative to each source's own oracle asks a different question.

## How much ordinary calibration buys a transferable prediction

In a symmetric rank-one calibration family a fresh fair sign $H$ emits old, new and target signs independently with conditional means $\alpha H,\beta H,\gamma H$. Flip the common sign convention so $\alpha\ge0$. The crossed product means are $p=\alpha\beta,\ q=\alpha\gamma$. Old twins expose $v=\alpha^2$. For $v>0$, the deployment correlation is $r=pq/v$ and $\Pr(Y=1\mid D=d)=(1+dr)/2$. The crossed data alone cannot identify $r$. For example $(\alpha,\beta,\gamma)=(1/2,1/2,1/2)$ and $(3/4,1/3,1/3)$ agree on both full crossed laws and give $r=1/4$ and $1/9$.

**PROVED, calibration price at weak old-view amplitude.** For fixed interior nonzero $b,c$, compare $(a,b,c)$ and $(2a,b/2,c/2)$ as $a\downarrow0$. Their desired correlations differ by $3bc/4$ while their entire crossed experiments agree. On each fresh old-only twin panel the product has mean $a^2$ or $4a^2$ and the remaining fair sign has identical conditional distribution. The two-panel-law KL is $O(a^4)$ per reset by the Bernoulli relative-entropy bound $D(p\|q)\le(p-q)^2/[q(1-q)]$. The chain rule, followed by data processing to a two-world decision correct with probability $1-\delta$ in each, requires KL at least $(1-2\delta)\log((1-\delta)/\delta)$. Hence $\Omega(a^{-4}\log(1/\delta))$ expected fresh twin panels are necessary for an error in $r$ smaller than half the gap, for $\delta\le1/4$. Under $|\alpha|\ge a$, estimating the two crossed means to a small constant times $a$ and the twin mean to a small constant times $a^2$ gives matching order for any fixed desired correlation accuracy. Hoeffding concentration and $|p|,|q|\le|\alpha|$ control the division.

If many old reads may be bought on one persistent reset, its signed mean concentrates around $\alpha H$. Taking the absolute value estimates $\alpha$ to fixed relative precision in $O(a^{-2}\log(1/\delta))$ reports, and the crossed means cost the same order in fresh panels. For the matching lower bound, reveal the old-only calibration signs $H$ for free. Every old-only read then has $O(a^2)$ conditional KL in the two worlds, regardless of adaptive stopping or reset choice. Fresh crossed panels still have zero KL. This proves the lower bound for this interface. Allowing repeated $D$, repeated $Y$, joint $D,Y$, or labelled latent signs on the crossed panels supplies different experiments. Accuracy and testing have this price even when some weak positive gain can be earned without calibration.

Given sampling intervals for $p,q,\alpha$, intersect with $p=\alpha\beta,q=\alpha\gamma$ and the unit amplitude constraints. For $\alpha>0$, the extreme $\beta\gamma$ lie at corners of the two clipped intervals for $\beta$ and $\gamma$. Between consecutive breakpoints $\alpha=|p_{\rm endpoint}|,|q_{\rm endpoint}|$ each corner is constant or a constant times $\alpha^{-1}$ or $\alpha^{-2}$. Checking endpoints therefore gives the sharp extrema on this confidence set. When $\alpha=0$ is feasible, all deployment correlations are feasible there. For an agreement-probability interval $[l,u]$ with $l<u$, the forecast minimizing worst conditional KL satisfies $\operatorname{logit}(q_*)=[h(l)-h(u)]/(u-l)$ for binary entropy $h$. Convexity in the true probability and monotonicity in the forecast give the result by equating the two endpoint losses. Point estimates and finite-precision releases can then be scored under the actual deployment law. [Registered experiment and physical replay](EVIDENCE.md#transferring-a-prediction-without-joint-labels)

## Learning a categorical assignment from disjoint pairs

Let the old, new and target alphabets have sizes $m,n,k$. Keep conditional independence, independently purchased twins and common reset weights, and suppose the source has at most $mn$ positive latent atoms. For view $A$, let $A^*(h)$ be a most probable emitted label, with ties broken consistently. The average probability $e_A$ of differing from $A^*$ is bounded by the independent twin disagreement $\delta_A$, since $\sum_i p_i^2\le\max_i p_i$ on each reset. Consequently all rounded $(T^*,D^*)$ cells are positive if each measured population $TD$ cell exceeds $\delta_T+\delta_D$. Exactly one positive atom then belongs to each $(t,d)$, and its rounded target defines some unknown assignment $Y^*=g_t(d)$. The learner receives neither its values nor a dictionary of transformations.

**PROVED, a finite-data certificate.** Suppose ordinary sample cells for $TD,TY$ have simultaneous errors bounded by $\eta$ and independent-twin disagreements have upper bounds $b_A$. Test for positive coverage with $\min_{td}\widehat P_{TD}(t,d)>\eta+b_T+b_D$. Put $b=b_T+b_D+b_Y$. For each $t$ retain all functions $g_t$ satisfying for every $y$, with $S_y=\{d:g_t(d)=y\}$,
$$
\left|\widehat P_{TY}(t,y)-\sum_{d\in S_y}\widehat P_{TD}(t,d)\right|
\ \le\ (1+|S_y|)\eta+b.
$$
The true rounded mapping passes. Couple all three raw views on one reset. Whenever all three equal their MAP labels, $Y=g_T(D)$. Thus the probability that the two events inside the difference disagree before sampling is at most $e_T+e_D+e_Y\le b$. Adding the cell sampling errors gives the test. A single surviving assignment for each row therefore identifies its rounded target throughout the compatible class. Tied partitions can leave multiple assignments even in noiseless population data. Enumeration costs $m k^n$ assignment evaluations; this can be prohibitive for large alphabets.

The learned assignment induces $Q(d,y)=\sum_{t:g_t(d)=y}P_{TD}(t,d)$ with the correct $D$ marginal and total variation from the true $P_{DY}$ at most $b$ by the same coupling. Pushing the empirical $TD$ law forward instead gives $\widehat Q$ at distance at most $\epsilon=\min(1,b+mn\eta/2)$. This bound averages over the observed contexts. To control next-symbol log loss without assuming every $D$ is frequent, smooth the *joint* by $R=(1-\epsilon)\widehat Q+\epsilon U$ with $U$ uniform over the $J=nk$ cells, and deploy $R(Y\mid D)$. For $0<\epsilon\le1/2$ the mean conditional KL is at most $(1+\ln J)\epsilon$. Indeed put $s=\mathrm{TV}(P_{DY},\widehat Q)$ and write $P_{DY}=(1-s)M+sA$, $\widehat Q=(1-s)M+sB$. Apply log-sum to the two terms of $P_{DY}$ and the lower bound $R\ge(1-\epsilon)(1-s)M+\epsilon U$. The joint KL is at most $(1-s)\ln[1/(1-\epsilon)]+s\ln(s/\epsilon)+s\ln J$. This last expression is convex in $s\in[0,\epsilon]$ and has its larger endpoint at $\epsilon$ since $J(1-\epsilon)\ge1$ for $J\ge2$. Using $-(1-\epsilon)\ln(1-\epsilon)\le\epsilon$ and bounding conditional KL by joint KL gives the result. Use the equality of the original laws if $\epsilon=0$. This gives an observable guarantee for every world satisfying the capacity premise.

At fixed alphabets this linear order in residual noise is necessary, even if the rounded assignment is identified exactly. Take $T$ constant, let deterministic $D$ label three atoms of masses $(1-2z,z,z)$ with $0<z<1/3$, and emit $Y=1$ with chance $1/4$ on just one of the two rare atoms and zero elsewhere. Swapping the rare atoms gives identical measured panels including all self twins. The population rounding certificate identifies $g\equiv0$ because the target mass $z/4$ and disagreement bound $3z/8$ exclude any nonempty preimage of one. An unsmoothed zero forecast has infinite population log loss. At a particular rare $D$ the two worlds differ by $1/4$ in total variation independently of $z$. Averaging their risks shows every common predictor incurs at least $z[D_{\rm KL}(\mathrm{Ber}(0)\|\mathrm{Ber}(1/8))+D_{\rm KL}(\mathrm{Ber}(1/4)\|\mathrm{Ber}(1/8))]$ in one world. Only an averaged error can vanish at this rate without further restrictions on context mass.

The capacity assumption is independently necessary. Let $T$ be constant, deterministic $D$ have masses $(1/3,2/3)$, and $\Pr(Y=0)=1/3$, with all twins perfect. A two-atom source must assign $Y=0$ exactly at $D=0$. A three-atom source equally weighted on $(D,Y)=(0,1),(1,0),(1,1)$ has all the same available observations and gives the opposite prediction at $D=0$. A learner that selects the least hidden cardinality explaining training alone cannot certify that its deployment prediction belongs to the actual source.

## How much a finite number of sampling contexts can teach

Let $S_1,\ldots,S_n$ be independent, fair unknown binary labels. Acquisition may choose adaptively among $m$ fixed contexts. Given the source $S$, chosen context $j$ and the entire preceding transcript, each purchased target is Bernoulli with mean $p_j(S)$. Auxiliary observations are allowed when their conditional law given the history and action is common to all sources. Fixed channel-design randomness independent of $S$ may be conditioned on. Suppose $B$ bounds the expected total target purchases. After acquisition a uniformly drawn independent name $D$ is revealed and the learner predicts $Y=S_D$. Let $G$ be its expected log gain over the fair forecast in nats per first deployment label. This workload measures the ability to use individually addressable distinctions after training.

**PROVED, predictive information bound.** For every acquisition policy and predictor,
$$
G\le\min\left\{\ln2,\frac{B\ln2}{n},
                     \frac{m}{2n}\ln(1+4B/m)\right\}.
$$
Write $Z$ for the acquisition transcript. The optimal log forecast gains $\sum_i I(S_i;Z)/n\le I(S;Z)/n$ by independence of the prior coordinates and subadditivity of conditional entropy. Each binary target costs at most $\ln 2$ in information, and the auxiliary kernels add zero. For the sharper upper bound use the same policy, stopping and auxiliary kernels with a separate half-count Bernoulli forecast in each context, giving a common reference law $Q$. With $N_j$ purchases, the path likelihood ratio to $Q$ is at most $\prod_j M_{N_j}$, $M_b=4^b/\binom{2b}{b}$. The one-arm maximum occurs on an all-equal stream as proved in the universal-code calculation below the earlier coin model. By induction $M_b\le\sqrt{4b+1}$ since $M_{b+1}/M_b=2(b+1)/(2b+1)$ and $(4b+5)(2b+1)^2-4(b+1)^2(4b+1)=1$. Now $I(S;Z)\le\mathbb E_S D(P_{Z\mid S}\|Q)$, and Jensen's inequality over contexts and realized counts gives $(m/2)\ln(1+4B/m)$. Truncate before any finite horizon and take the increasing limit of transcript information to include adaptive stopping. [Half-count path proof](#a-shared-residual-for-discarded-bernoulli-hypotheses) · [Krichevsky and Trofimov](https://doi.org/10.1109/TIT.1981.1056331)

The full-history condition matters. Two streams can return $U_i$ and $U_i\mathbin{\mathrm{xor}}S_i$ on corresponding reads for independent fair $U_i$. Each stream alone appears iid and fair even given $S$. Reading both discloses one exact label per pair. Reprogramming a single live slot successively to emit each individual $S_i$ also reveals the labels in $n$ reads. The theorem counts distinct stationary experiments used, not concurrently occupied physical slots. A supplied prior correlating all labels can let one response predict every label. The independent fair prior gives an average-source upper bound and hence an upper bound on attainable worst-source gain, with a separate achievability question in each case.

**PROVED, matching average-source construction from ordinary observations.** For $m$ disjoint equal-sized groups of $q=n/m$ names, use one fixed context per group. A reset privately selects rank $j\in\{0,\ldots,q-1\}$ with probability $2^j/M$, $M=2^q-1$, and emits the selected name or its binary target on separate resets. In a target-only draw the Bernoulli mean is $J/M$ where $J=\sum_j2^j S_j$ is uniform on $0,\ldots,M$. The multiset of weights and named groups may be known, with an arbitrary unknown permutation of name ranks in each. Neither the hidden name of a target read nor its target on a name read is supplied. Counts of $L\ge1$ independent draws per type and per group suffice for the following simple learner. Sort the name counts, round $M$ times the empirical target fraction to the nearest integer, decode its bits, and attenuate each forecast using the bounds below.

Put $\sigma=M/\sqrt L$. For the bit at true rank $j$, a rounding error can occur only by crossing an internal half-integer boundary spaced $2^j$ apart. At each boundary there are at most two integer values $J$ at distance $v+1/2$ for a given integer $v\ge0$. Averaging one-sided Hoeffding bounds over uniform $J$, the bit error is at most
$$
2^{1-j}\sum_{v\ge0}\exp[-2(v+1/2)^2/\sigma^2]
\le 2^{1-j}\min\left\{\sigma\sqrt{\pi/2},
\frac{\exp[-1/(2\sigma^2)]}{1-\exp[-4/\sigma^2]}\right\}.
$$
For the first bound on the sum integrate the decreasing Gaussian on each $[v,v+1/2]$. For the second use $(v+1/2)^2\ge1/4+2v$. If all empirical name probabilities differ by less than $2^j/(4M)$ from their truths, the true label at rank $j$ retains that rank, since every competing mass differs by at least $2^j/(2M)$. The expected squared Euclidean error of all multinomial probabilities sums to at most $1/L$. Markov bounds the rank error by $16(\sigma2^{-j})^2$, including unsampled names. Add the two bounds and denote the result by $v_j$. For the name installed at rank $j$, predict the decoded bit with probability $1-u_j$ for $u_j=\min(1/2,v_j)$. If $u_j=1/2$ the log loss is $\ln2$ regardless of error; otherwise its expectation is at most $h(u_j)$, the binary entropy.

Once $j$ exceeds $\max(0,\lceil\log_2\sigma\rceil)$ by $k\ge0$, $u_j\le\min(1/2,C2^{-k})$ for an absolute constant $C$. The sum of the corresponding entropies is bounded independently of $q,L$, since $h(x)\le x(1-\ln x)$. Thus the gain per uniform fresh name is at least
$$
\min\{\ln2,\ (m/(2n))\ln(1+L)\}-O(m/n).
$$
Indeed at most $\min(q,\max(0,\lceil\log_2\sigma\rceil))$ low bits have been charged the full $\ln2$. Subtract their cost and the bounded tail from $q\ln 2$, and use $M=2^q-1$. With $B=mL$ target reports and $B$ additional name-only reports, this matches the preceding general upper bound to additive $O(m/n)$, uniformly in budget. It is an existence result for an implementable sampling design with an unknown mapping. Different fixed contexts can all be uninformative. The learner sorts $q$ counts and uses integer arithmetic. A simulator for the geometric context can select the hidden rank using the number of consecutive zeros before the first fair one, taken modulo $q$ in reverse rank order. Summing its geometric series gives exactly $2^j/M$ with mean two fair flips per reset and one private label access. An implementation still needs to store and access the unknown source labels. The predictor receives only its purchased report. Classical detecting families recover subsets from exact intersection counts. A single sample from a mixture supplies a different amount of information from one exact count. [Lindström](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/6E7EE9DAABF64BF4DF713B20C87F7C8F/S0008439500054813a.pdf/on-a-combinatorial-problem-in-number-theory.pdf) · [Cantor and Mills](https://www.cambridge.org/core/services/aop-cambridge-core/content/view/3C5AA2421EC1EE60B061DE41FDA824A4/S0008414X00040037a.pdf/div-class-title-determination-of-a-subset-from-certain-combinatorial-properties-div.pdf)

**PROVED, worst-source limitation.** For one noiseless geometric group with $q\ge2$, the two adjacent integers $(M-1)/2,(M+1)/2$ encode complementary label vectors. They disagree at every possible deployment input, while their Bernoulli observations have per-target KL $M^{-1}\ln[(M+1)/(M-1)]=\Theta(M^{-2})$. Independent name-only calibration carries zero information between these hypotheses. For an equal prior over this pair, the minimum *sum* of $q$ next-label losses is $qH(W\mid Z)$ since each label is the same unknown source bit $W$ up to a known flip. This is at least $2q\ln2$ times the optimal testing error, using $h(x)\ge2\ln2\min(x,1-x)$. For $L$ target reports the affinity of their laws is $(1-M^{-2})^{L/2}$. Cauchy–Schwarz gives $\mathrm{TV}\le\sqrt{1-\mathrm{affinity}^2}$ and hence testing error at least $(1-M^{-2})^L/4$. Uniformly bounded total label loss as $q$ grows requires $\Omega(M^2\ln q)$ reports. On the product prior the simple decoder already gives bounded average total loss at $L=M^2$. Thus an exact or close aggregate fit can still conceal an expensive disagreement on every deployment token.

There are several ways to change the worst-case conclusion and each prices a different physical operation. If the source permits a known independently fair XOR mask on every hidden coordinate *before* sampling its target, every fixed label vector has exactly the preceding average risk after unmasking. A learner cannot obtain those independent flips by toggling an aggregate bit after its hidden coordinate has been discarded. Alternatively, if a context can present any function of the full label vector as a Bernoulli mean, index the vectors by a reflected Gray path $g(0),\ldots,g(M)$, whose adjacent names differ in one coordinate. Set the mean for $s$ to $g^{-1}(s)/M$. With $L=M^2$ round to $\hat J$ and predict the bit marginals of $Q(k)\propto\exp(-|k-\hat J|)$. Its central mass is at least a fixed $c>0$. Its expected Hamming distance to $g(\hat J)$ is bounded by its expected index distance, an absolute constant. For all true bits matching the center, the sum of log losses is bounded by $c^{-1}$ times that expected distance. At most $d=|J-\hat J|$ true bits mismatch and each is assigned probability at least $Q(J)\ge c e^{-d}$. Their sum loss is at most $d(d-\ln c)$. Rounding and binomial variance give $\mathbb E d^2\le2M^2/(4L)+1/2=1$ uniformly in $J$. The worst expected sum loss is therefore bounded independently of $q$. The encoder must compute an inverse Gray word, which involves prefix parities; a single private lookup returning the sampled raw label alone implements the geometric code. [Registered experiment, comparator and physical replay](EVIDENCE.md#how-many-named-labels-can-aggregate-ordinary-reports-teach)

## When a fitted aggregate conditional predicts the next word

Let $A=P_{TY}$, $\nu=P_Y$, $J=P_{DY}$. A randomized categorical tag on $K\ge2$ labels copies $D$ with probability $\rho$ and otherwise draws a fresh uniform label independent of $(D,Y)$. Write $u$ for the uniform column vector. Separate ordinary $TD$ and $TY$ panels give
$$
\rho=\frac{K\Pr(T=D)-1}{K-1},\qquad
A=\rho J+(1-\rho)u\nu^\top,\qquad J=u\nu^\top+(A-u\nu^\top)/\rho
$$
for $\rho>0$. At $\rho=0$ only the marginals survive. This observable calibration uses the match rate for unknown $\rho$. Disclosing which trial copied the input, supplying common row IDs or letting the learner reconstruct chronology changes the experiment. A sampler that generates the tag from an actual word has access which the recipient of aggregate tables lacks.

**PROVED, fixed-alphabet resource prices.** For independent panels with $L$ observations each, a small joint total-variation tolerance $\epsilon$ at fixed confidence costs order $1/(\rho^2\epsilon^2)$ in the nondegenerate worst case. Concentration of the finitely many cells and the copy-rate estimate followed by the displayed inversion gives the upper order. The calibration error in the numerator multiplies the bounded difference $u\widehat\nu^\top-J$. Clip and normalize the estimated joint when needed. For the lower bound take fair binary $D,Y$ with correlations $\pm c\epsilon$ for a constant $c>2$. The $TD$ panel is identical, and a $TY$ pair has correlation $\pm\rho c\epsilon$ and KL of order $\rho^2\epsilon^2$. A fixed-confidence distinction between separated joints costs the reciprocal order by data processing.

For *mean conditional log loss* over $D$, the sharp fixed-confidence order over the whole simplex at small accuracy $\epsilon$ is
$$
L\asymp \frac1{\rho\epsilon}+\frac{1-\rho}{\rho^2\epsilon^2}.
$$
Constants may depend on the fixed alphabets and confidence. Put $r=1/(\rho L)+\sqrt{1-\rho}/(\rho\sqrt L)$. For each cell, the variable $1_{\{T=t,Y=y\}}-(1-\rho)u_t1_{\{Y=y\}}$ has mean $\rho J_{ty}$ and variance bounded by $C(\rho J_{ty}+1-\rho)$. Bernstein bounds its empirical mean. The independent match panel estimates $\rho$ with error $O(\sqrt{(1-\rho)/L}+1/L)$ using the mismatch event. Where $r$ is small the estimated rate is at least $\rho/2$ on the simultaneous high-probability event. The signed deconvolved cell $V_i$ then has error at most $C(\sqrt{J_i/(\rho L)}+r)$. With unknown $\rho$, estimate an upper bound on the mismatch mean by a constant times its empirical frequency plus $C/L$ and a lower bound on $\rho$ using the ensuing Bernstein radius. Chernoff and Bernstein give an observable conservative padding radius $\tilde r$ between $r$ and a fixed multiple of $r$ in this range. Return uniform on an uninformative confidence interval.

Set $e_i=\max(0,V_i)+C'\tilde r$ with a sufficiently large constant. If $J_i$ exceeds a large multiple of $r$, the cell error is less than $J_i/2$; otherwise the padding supplies its lower bound. Hence $e_i\ge c(J_i+r)$ and $(e_i-J_i)^2/e_i=O(r)$ per cell. After normalization, joint KL is at most $\sum_i(e_i-J_i)^2/e_i$ by $\ln x\le x-1$, and conditional KL is bounded by joint KL. For necessity take fair $D$ and make $Y$ flip it with probability $0$ or $t$. Their conditional KL radius is at least $h(t/2)-h(t)/2=(\ln2)t/2+O(t^2)$ by averaging over the two worlds. The observed $TY$ mismatch probabilities are $(1-\rho)/2$ and $(1-\rho)/2+\rho t$. Their one-pair KL in the forward direction is at most $C\rho^2 t^2/(1-\rho+\rho t)$ by the Bernoulli chi-square bound. The other panel is identical. Choosing $t$ a suitable multiple of $\epsilon$ and testing proves the lower order, including the clean endpoint $\rho=1$. This is a property of an available statistically smoothed estimator. The empirical positive-part comparator has its measured score in the text experiment.

For an arbitrary old tag let $W=P(D\mid T)$ and let $B=P(Y\mid T)$ and $\Theta=P(Y\mid D)$ denote matrices with conditioning values as rows. Define the structural discrepancy $E=B-W\Theta$ and the fitted discrepancy $F=B-W\widehat\Theta$. A learner fitting the ordinary crossed target likelihood sees $F$ when $W,B$ are known. The correct deployment conditional also requires control of $E$. On positive marginals put $M=\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{P_T})W\operatorname{diag}(1/\sqrt{P_D})$. If $M$ has full column rank, then
$$
\|\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{P_D})(\widehat\Theta-\Theta)\|_F
\le \frac{\|\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{P_T})E\|_F+
                \|\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{P_T})F\|_F}{\sigma_{\min}(M)} .
$$
This follows by subtracting the two discrepancies. Conditional Jensen, $\|p-q\|_2^2\le 2\,\mathrm{TV}(p,q)^2$ and Pinsker bound the squared first numerator norm by $I(T;Y\mid D)$, and the second by the weighted aggregate KL. A positive prediction floor $\gamma$ gives an upper bound on downstream KL by the squared right side divided by $\gamma$. Estimated matrices add sampling error. The randomized channel sets $E=0$ in the population. Full rank or an accurate aggregate fit alone leaves the structural discrepancy free.

For an explicit counterexample, the strictly positive fair sign laws $P_c(t,d,y)=(1+td/4+ty/8+c\,dy)/8$ for $c=-1/2,0,1/2$ have the same crossed panels and invertible $W$. The exactly fitting aggregate conditional agrees with $D$ with probability $3/4$. The true agreement probabilities are $1/4,1/2,3/4$ respectively. The first world pays excess $(\ln3)/2$ nats per forecast. This example concerns general tags. If $T,D,Y$ are three consecutive values under the same token map, their chronological overlap supplies another observation. On any contiguous window of $N$ triples, the full $TD$ and $DY$ count matrices differ by exactly one entering and one leaving pair, giving TV at most $1/N$. Under stationarity their population joint laws are identical. A separate random subset of the $TD$ table thus estimates a shifted training bigram law even when the factorization $B=W\Theta$ fails.

Failure of that factorization can itself be stationary. The positive triple law $(1+td/4-ty/8+dy/4)/8$ has equal consecutive-pair marginals and therefore extends to a stationary order-two chain by using its $P(y\mid t,d)$ and starting from its pair marginal. The exact factorized fit predicts match probability $1/4$, while the true adjacent-pair prediction has match probability $5/8$. The excess is .31275 nats despite a perfect aggregate fit. In the ordinary text experiment, the shift identity was checked before scoring and supplied an additional directly available comparator. [Frozen text forecasts and raw tests](EVIDENCE.md#learning-an-ordinary-next-word-table-from-two-unpaired-panels)

## How much of a joint law survives two matched histograms?

A matched bag contains $b$ independently drawn *pairs* on a common source. The learner receives their two unordered marginal histograms, with a common bag identifier but without the internal item pairing. For independent centered features $f,g$ across items (the two features of one item may be dependent), the product of the two bag sums divided by $b$ estimates $\mathbb E[fg]$. Independent bags for the two views lose this covariance. The following calculation quantifies what the matched counts disclose.

**PROVED, one bag at weak dependence.** Take fair sign pairs with $\mathbb E[DY]=c$. Under the independent null the ordered likelihood ratio is $\prod_{i=1}^b(1+cD_iY_i)$. Conditional expectation given the two counts symmetrizes the two coordinate strings separately. For degree $j$, let $K_j(D)=\binom bj^{-1/2}\sum_{|A|=j}\prod_{i\in A}D_i$ and define $K_j(Y)$ similarly. The signs are fair under the null, so the normalized polynomials are orthonormal in each count. Each individual degree-$j$ product projects to $K_j/\sqrt{\binom bj}$. The count likelihood ratio is exactly $\sum_{j=0}^b c^j K_j(D)K_j(Y)$. In particular
$$
\chi^2(P_c\|P_0)=\sum_{j=1}^b c^{2j},\qquad \mathcal I_b(0)=1 .
$$
The Fisher information in $b$ aligned complete pairs is $b$. For fixed $|c|<1$ the displayed sum is bounded uniformly in bag size. At $|c|=1$ it equals $b$, so extrapolation from weak dependence to singular sources changes the answer.

The first score $U=(\sum D_i)(\sum Y_i)/b$ has mean $c$ and variance $1+(1-2/b)c^2$. For the second moment expand the squared sums, keeping the constant $b^2$ and $2b(b-1)$ terms with matched signs of mean $c^2$. A median of independent block means thus estimates $c$ to $\epsilon$ at confidence $1-\delta$ with $O(\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta))$ bags. Conversely, testing zero against $3\epsilon$ needs that many bags, since each bag has KL at most $\log(1+\chi^2)\le-\log(1-9\epsilon^2)$ and data processing gives the Bernoulli testing lower bound. This survives adaptive choices of bag size made before each fresh bag. At fixed $b$ the weak-correlation minimax raw cost is $\Theta(2b\epsilon^{-2}\log(1/\delta))$ sign observations. The collector pays for every item.

For categorical pairs on supported marginals $p,q$, let $\sigma_1,\ldots,\sigma_r$ be the nonconstant singular values of $\operatorname{diag}(p)^{-1/2}P_{DY}\operatorname{diag}(q)^{-1/2}$. The same count laws satisfy
$$
1+\chi^2=\sum_{\substack{k_1,\ldots,k_r\ge0\\\sum k_i\le b}}\prod_i\sigma_i^{2k_i}.
$$
Use left and right orthonormal mean-zero functions for the nonconstant terms of the one-pair density. In the $b$-fold expansion, each allocation of degrees to these features projects to a symmetrized tensor. For fixed multiplicities the distinct allocations are orthogonal; the square root of their number normalizes the two tensor sums, just as in the sign case. Products at distinct multiplicities are orthogonal, giving the formula. Thus at independence the Fisher information in a zero-row/column-marginal perturbation $H$ is $\sum_{dy}H_{dy}^2/(p_dq_y)$ per bag for every $b$. Information about unknown *marginals* can still grow with $b$. These expansions use established multivariate Krawtchouk and Lancaster theory; the downstream observation price follows from their likelihood ratios. [Diaconis and Griffiths](https://arxiv.org/abs/1309.0112)

## What a random partition of one fixed tape can identify

On an arbitrary realized tape of $N$ paired values, no independence across positions is needed. Take a uniform random equal-bag partition with $b\mid N$, $1\le b<N$ and $N\ge4$. Center scalar input and output features $x_i,y_i$ by their tape means. Let $C=N^{-1}\sum_i x_i y_i$ and let $X_g,Y_g$ be their sums in bag $g$. The learner can center counts using pooled marginal histograms. Then
$$
\widehat C=\frac{N-1}{N(N-b)}\sum_g X_gY_g
$$
is design-unbiased. Each pair of different indices is in the same bag with probability $p=(b-1)/(N-1)$, and $\sum_{i\ne j}x_i y_j=-NC$. Applying this to every pair of class indicators and adding the product of the tape marginals gives an estimator of the complete raw joint table.

There is an exact variance. Put $h_{ij}=x_i y_j+x_j y_i$ for $i<j$. Subtract from $h_{ij}$ its least-squares projection onto $a_i+a_j$ to obtain $g_{ij}$ with each vertex sum zero. The projection exists in the finite Euclidean edge space; for degrees $s_i=\sum_{j\ne i}h_{ij}$ one can take $a_i=[s_i-\sum_k s_k/(2(N-1))]/(N-2)$. Because each vertex has $b-1$ bag neighbors the removed term has deterministic bag total. In the residual quadratic form, the sums over ordered pairs of adjacent and disjoint distinct edges are respectively $-2\sum g_{ij}^2$ and $\sum g_{ij}^2$. This follows by expanding the zero vertex sums and then the zero total sum. The probabilities that adjacent and disjoint edges are both included are respectively $p_1=p(b-2)/(N-2)$ and $p_2=p[(b-2)(b-3)+(N-b)(b-1)]/[(N-2)(N-3)]$. Combining $p-2p_1+p_2$ and the estimator's normalization gives
$$
\operatorname{Var}(\widehat C)=
\frac{(N-1)(b-1)}{N^2(N-b)(N-3)}\sum_{i<j}g_{ij}^2
\le\frac{(N-1)(b-1)}{(N-3)(N-b)}
       [\operatorname{Var}_{\rm tape}(x)\operatorname{Var}_{\rm tape}(y)+C^2].
$$
For the inequality use $\sum g^2\le\sum h^2\le(\sum x_i^2)(\sum y_i^2)+(\sum x_i y_i)^2$. Balanced $x=y\in\{-1,1\}$ gives exactly $2(b-1)(N-2)/[(N-b)(N-3)]$. Separate independent repartitions of the same tape reduce variance but require additional aligned acquisitions.

**PROVED, minimax fixed-tape price.** Over all fixed sign tapes with a single random partition, mean-squared error for covariance has order $(b-1)/(N-b)$ even allowing biased estimators. The upper bound is above. For the lower bound add the known tape-mean product to turn a covariance estimate into one for the empirical uncentered moment. First take independent iid sign tapes as a prior on the fixed input. In one bag the unobserved sum of products has variance $b$ and its conditional expectation is the product of the observed sums divided by $b$, with variance one at independence. So posterior expected residual variance in the empirical tape moment is $(b-1)/(bN)$. This lower bound gives the required order for bounded $b$. Next consider iid pairs of correlation zero or $c$ of order $1/\sqrt{N/b}$. The chi-square bound on each bag and the two-point test above lower-bound worst iid mean-squared error for $c$ by $c_0/(N/b)$ for an absolute $c_0>0$. A fixed-tape estimator of worst mean-squared error $R$ would give iid risk at most $2R+2/N$ by the variance of the actual tape mean. Thus $R\ge c_0b/(2N)-1/N$, of order $b/N$ for sufficiently large $b$. Since a proper divisor $b$ is at most $N/2$, the two regimes prove the claim. Singleton bags exactly recover the empirical joint. A single whole-tape bag deterministically reveals its two empirical marginals and can leave opposite empirical associations. A single long *iid* bag instead has random totals and the count experiment derived above.

On sequential text, contiguous bags of previous and current words differ in their two class histograms by at most one entering and one leaving token. In contrast, randomly regrouping the very same positions isolates the within-position covariance in expectation. Both interfaces retain the same lexical and class marginals. A learner that ignores the grouping operation can infer a strong copying rule and increase its actual next-word loss. [Frozen comparison](EVIDENCE.md#learning-next-word-predictions-from-matched-bag-histograms)

## What two separated observers must communicate to learn a conditional

Two sites hold arbitrary aligned categorical tapes $(D_i)_{i=1}^N$ and $(Y_i)_{i=1}^N$. Their alphabets have fixed sizes at least two. They may use public randomness independent of the data, shared indices, arbitrary local computation, and interactive messages with a worst-case bit cap. Release a conditional prediction table usable without further remote access. Score it against the empirical conditional on the fixed tapes, averaging over the empirical $D$ marginal. For fixed success probability $2/3$ on every tape, small $\epsilon$ and large $N$, the necessary and sufficient communication is
$$
\Theta(\min\{N,\epsilon^{-1}\})\quad\text{for mean conditional KL}\le\epsilon,\qquad
\Theta(\min\{N,\epsilon^{-2}\})\quad\text{for mean conditional TV}\le\epsilon .
$$
The same TV order holds for joint TV. Constants depend on the alphabets. This compares all protocols under the stated data access.

**Upper bounds.** Sample $k$ shared indices independently and uniformly with replacement. Only their two categorical values need be sent. With $J$ joint cells, let $p_i$ be each empirical population mass and $Z_i$ its sample count. Fit the add-one joint $\widehat p_i=(Z_i+1)/(k+J)$ and condition it. The binomial identity $\mathbb E[1/(Z_i+1)]=[1-(1-p_i)^{k+1}]/[(k+1)p_i]$ at $p_i>0$ follows by summing the binomial coefficients with the index shifted by one. Jensen therefore gives $\mathbb E D(p\|\widehat p)\le\ln[(k+J)/(k+1)]$. The chain rule bounds mean conditional KL. Markov gives a fixed-confidence upper bound at $k=O(\epsilon^{-1})$; applying Pinsker and Jensen gives the TV result at $O(\epsilon^{-2})$. Sending all values gives the exact-census option. Finite rational count packets require only $O(J\log(k+1))$ further bits if the table is published by a third party. Quantizing every prediction to an unchanging grid can impose a positive error floor.

**Lower bounds, a reduction from Gap-Hamming.** Give one party a sign string $x$ and the other $y$ of length $v$, with Hamming distance respectively below $v/2-\sqrt v$ or above $v/2+\sqrt v$. Their empirical sign correlation therefore differs from zero by at least $2/\sqrt v$ with opposite signs. Double both strings by appending their negations. This keeps the correlation and makes both sign marginals public. Repeat the $2v$-position construction $\lfloor N/(2v)\rfloor$ times, then fill any remaining positions with public $(+,+)$ values. For $v\le N/2$ at least half of the resulting tape is repeated hard input. The empirical correlation lies on opposite sides of the known padding contribution, with a deviation at least $1/\sqrt v$ in each case. The released table and public input marginal compute a correlation approximation with error at most $2\epsilon$ for conditional TV, or $\sqrt{2\epsilon}$ for conditional KL by Pinsker and Jensen. Choosing $v$ a sufficiently small constant times $\min(N,\epsilon^{-2})$ or $\min(N,\epsilon^{-1})$ decides the hard problem. Chakrabarti–Regev's randomized communication lower bound is $\Omega(v)$ even with interaction. A referee can be simulated at a collector or can evaluate the released table directly. Joint TV similarly bounds correlation error by twice TV. [Chakrabarti and Regev, Theorem 2.6](https://arxiv.org/pdf/1009.3460)

Consecutive text with the same input and output class map has extra local structure. With full chronological access the input collector already knows $Y_i=D_{i+1}$ for $i<N$. A single boundary symbol yields the exact empirical conditional locally. Even withholding that symbol, placing uniform target mass for the missing item costs at most $\ln|Y|/N$ in mean KL and $(1-1/|Y|)/N$ in TV by convexity of mixtures with the common $N-1$ items. For several disjoint runs count one boundary per run. An external recipient can receive exact joint counts in $O(J\log(N+1))$ bits. The arbitrary-tape lower bound is therefore inapplicable to unrestricted full chronological text. The word experiment compares specific histogram and public-sampling messages to the same receiving learner and charges their actual acquisition permissions. [Equal-message-budget word scores](EVIDENCE.md#shared-position-sampling-at-the-same-communication-budget)

## A certificate for learning a computation from distributed outcomes

A receiver knows descriptor rows $D_1,\ldots,D_N$ and a class of candidate deterministic binary programs. A sender knows only the aligned ordinary outcome bits $Y\in\mathbb F_2^N$. Assume public random masks independent of both tapes, and let the sender release prefixes of $RY$ for independent uniform rows of a binary matrix $R$. The receiver can compute the same checks on any candidate's predicted tape $f(D)$. Give the candidates prefix-code lengths $\ell(f)$ with $\sum_f2^{-\ell(f)}\le1$. The catalogue may depend on the known descriptors but must be fixed independently of the verification masks, including all the choices a later search can make.

**PROVED.** With probability at least $1-\delta$, simultaneously for every candidate, agreement on at least $\lceil\ell(f)+\log_2(1/\delta)\rceil$ prefix bits implies $f(D)=Y$. A nonzero residual has a fair inner product with every fresh row, so the probability of matching the required prefix is at most $\delta\,2^{-\ell(f)}$. Sum the probabilities. Adaptive search and stopping are covered because passing a longer prefix implies passing the fixed shorter one. This is a direct use of universal hashing and prefix coding. It certifies equality on the measured tape. Future names absent from the support, unobserved input histories, or programs agreeing there but differing elsewhere require further information. Programs encoding the realized mask for free would invalidate the bound. If an admissible generating candidate is actually found, the message cost can track its description length. Exhaustive program search can still be impractical. [Carter and Wegman, universal hashing](https://doi.org/10.1016/0022-0000(79)90044-8)

For a binary linear model $Y=A\theta$, the receiver forms $RA$ locally. Write $r=\operatorname{rank}A$. Its image after $t$ checks loses rank with probability at most $(2^r-1)2^{-t}$, by testing the nonzero prediction vectors. Conditional on full rank, binary elimination recovers the unique *prediction vector*. Identifying coefficients requires full column rank of $A$. For an unknown affine rule on all six-bit names, seven identifiable coefficients suffice. An unrestricted lookup on 64 observed names instead has 64 independent coefficients. One affine rule can also be written as a lookup. Certifying the shorter predictor describes its behavior and cost, without identifying which implementation the source used.

For up to one unknown errant report, add the candidate residuals $e=0,e_1,\ldots,e_N$. Distinct decompositions into a codeword and at most one error are possible precisely when the original code has distance at most two, for a nontrivial code. At distance at least three all radius-one balls are disjoint. With repeated names the minimum lookup-code distance is the smallest positive name count. For the receiver, reduce $RY$ and each $Re_i$ modulo the column span of $RA$. A unique compatible coset representative identifies the error, and elimination recovers the rule after subtraction. At most $2^r(N+1)$ possible corrected tapes give a per-fixed-tape upper budget of $r+\lceil\log_2(N+1)\rceil+\lceil\log_2(1/\delta)\rceil$ dense checks. Searching this class uses receiver work proportional to the error candidates and elimination cost. Requesting simultaneous success for every tape under one fixed random matrix changes the union bound. The code-length lower bound for reconstructing every possible *error position and rule* does not directly apply to recovering just the underlying rule.

There is a separate limitation on dense random checks. Let a uniform one of $2^r$ distinct rules induce $Y=A\theta+E$, with an independent uniform single flipped position. Conditional on the public matrix, the noise sketch is a uniformly selected column. Its expected collision probability is $1/N+(1-1/N)2^{-t}$. Shannon entropy dominates negative log collision probability, and Jensen and the $t$-bit output alphabet give
$$
I(\theta;RY\mid R)\le\log_2[1+(2^t-1)/N]\quad\text{bits}.
$$
A test or Fano bound then prices the missing position information even when the receiver only wants the rule *using this particular dense interface*. More generally for independent noise $E$, replace $1/N$ in this derivation by $\sum_eP(E=e)^2$. For iid flips at fixed rate $0<\eta<1/2$ this is $[(1-\eta)^2+\eta^2]^N$. Even one uniformly unknown constant bit needs $\Omega_\eta(N)$ dense checks for a fixed predictive advantage, while the sender could estimate a constant by majority and communicate a single bit. The computation available locally and the type of projection determine which rare errors disrupt learning. [Registered acquisition and clean-stream execution](EVIDENCE.md#learning-which-binary-computation-fits-a-separated-tape)

The variable thresholds in the dense experiment price the forward parity payload with feedback or an on-demand read interface. Four possible endpoints require at most three “continue/stop” bits from the receiver (no decision after the final endpoint), plus the agreed stage convention. With strictly one-way transmission and privately held descriptors, a sender following this implementation sends the maximum 86 bits and the receiver may use a shorter prefix. A target size known to both parties can use the corresponding fixed prefix in one direction.

## Recovering a clean lookup without locating the raw errors

Fix an ordered tape of $N$ public positions bearing $d$ privately held descriptor names on the receiver's side, with every name occurring at least $s=2h+1$ times, $h\ge1$. The other party holds the binary outcomes of an unknown arbitrary lookup $f$ with at most $h$ flipped positions. Each position independently chooses a uniform public bucket in $[t]$. The sender releases the $t$ parities of the outcomes by bucket. The receiver builds a $t\times d$ binary matrix $B$: the column for a name is the XOR of the unit vectors of the buckets at its occurrences. The received vector is $Bf+e$ with $|e|\le h$, even when several raw errors hit one bucket. Everything below conditions on a fixed descriptor tape independently of the hash.

**PROVED, with an independent proof attack.** Put $A=e_{\rm nat}^2(2h+2)^2d/(2t)$, where $e_{\rm nat}$ is the base of the natural logarithm. For $A<1$ the probability that any nonzero $u$ has $|Bu|\le2h$ is at most $A/(1-A)$. On the complementary event a single sketch recovers every lookup simultaneously against every allowed corruption of this tape. At fixed $h$ and constant confidence this takes $O(d)$ transmitted bits independent of the tape length.

To prove it, fix a support of $k\ge1$ names. Its image is a walk on the binary $t$-cube starting at zero after $L\ge(2h+1)k$ independent uniform coordinate flips. Write $p_L(w)$ for a *single* vertex probability at weight $w$. The recurrence is $t p_{L+1}(w)=w p_L(w-1)+(t-w)p_L(w+1)$. Subtract the value at $w+2$. The result is $w[p_L(w-1)-p_L(w+1)]+(t-w-2)[p_L(w+1)-p_L(w+3)]$; boundary terms with zero multipliers are omitted. Induction from the point mass at zero shows nonincreasing single-vertex probabilities within each parity. Thus among the vertices of attainable parity, the ball of radius $2h$ consists of the largest point masses up to ties. The two-step walk is doubly stochastic on this parity class. The sum of its largest $m$ masses cannot increase: the mass on any $m$ output vertices is a weighted sum of the previous masses with weights in $[0,1]$ summing to $m$. The ball probability therefore cannot increase when two steps are added.

Reduce $L$ to $L_0=(2h+1)k$ or $(2h+1)k+1$ with the same parity. Ending in the ball leaves at most $2h$ buckets of odd multiplicity. In any realization, $(L_0-|Bu|)/2$ disjoint equal-bucket pairs can be formed by pairing within each bucket. This is at least $k$ for $k\ge2$; for $k=1$ the parity bound gives at least one pair. There are at most $L_0^{2k}/(2^k k!)\le[e_{\rm nat} L_0^2/(2k)]^k$ choices of $k$ disjoint pairs. Each choice matches with probability $t^{-k}$. Since $L_0\le(2h+2)k$ and $\binom dk\le(e_{\rm nat}d/k)^k$, summing over all nonempty supports gives $\sum_{k=1}^d A^k\le A/(1-A)$. On success $B$ has full column rank and its code has distance at least $2h+1$. All radius-$h$ balls around codewords are disjoint. Binary elimination and a search over bucket error patterns recover the unique rule. Different raw errors may be indistinguishable, which is permissible for learning $f$.

**Sharpness for one iid bucket map.** Give each of the $d$ names exactly $s$ occurrences. Any internal bucket collision reduces its column weight to at most $s-2=2h-1$. Its support can be partitioned into two groups of size at most $h$; every occupied support bucket has a raw occurrence available to flip. The two lookup rules differing on that name can then produce the same sketch under allowed perturbations. A uniformly valid map must avoid internal collisions in every name. Its success probability is at most $[(t)_s/t^s]^d\le\exp[-d\,s(s-1)/(2t)]$, and it vanishes for $t<s$. For any prescribed longer $N$ with $d\ge2$, leave $d-1$ names at their minimum and place the extras in the last. Thus the worst-case one-hash price for the simultaneous certificate is $\Theta(dh^2/\delta)$ at failure $\delta\le1/2$. This lower bound is specific to that hash design and uniform success criterion. For arbitrary one-way protocols, the $2^d$ equiprobable clean rules alone need at least $d+\log_2(1-\delta)$ fixed-length bits to recover the entire table with probability $1-\delta$, since each message has just one decoded rule once the coins are fixed.

For higher confidence, independent constant-success hashes can all be sent one way. The receiver checks each map using descriptors alone and uses the first with a distance certificate. This uses $O((h+1)^2d\log(1/\delta))$ forward bits. With feedback the receiver can try public hashes locally until it finds a valid one, name that seed's index with a unary prefix code, then request one sketch. This gives zero decoding error, almost-sure termination, expected $O(1)$ reverse bits and expected constant trials, assuming a shared random stream. Each trial processes the descriptor tape. Raw reads, public randomness, verification, memory and any dissemination of a private name dictionary have separate costs. With unit vectors one needs $N\log_2t$ ideal public bits for power-of-two $t$ and a single pass over each tape. The sender needs $t$ parity bits and the receiver can store $td$ incidence bits. Full rank plus injectivity of the quotient syndromes of all $\sum_{i=0}^h\binom ti$ radius-$h$ errors exactly certifies the required distance: any nonzero codeword of weight at most $2h$ can be split into two such errors, and conversely. The tests can be expensive as $h$ grows. These are message-optimal orders at fixed $h$ and constant confidence for an arbitrary table, without a claim of optimal local computation.

At $h=0$, allowing just one occurrence per name changes the sparse result. If every name occurs once, all $d$ bucket unit columns must be distinct, requiring order $d^2$ buckets for constant success when $d\ge2$. If each name occurs at least twice, reaching zero from a support of $k$ needs $k$ disjoint equal pairs at the shortest attainable even walk, and the same proof gives $O(d/\delta)$ buckets. With exactly two occurrences per name an internal collision supplies the matching lower bound. Dense linear checks already avoid the singleton birthday obstruction in the noiseless case. The distinction concerns the observation protocol and its repetition promise.

## Which state does an ordinary rare token force the learner to construct?

Let named raw tokens toggle coordinates of $x\in\mathbb F_2^n$. A subsequent query yields an ordinary binary outcome with success probability $\eta+\kappa\,1_F(x)$, where $F=a+V$ is an unknown affine flat of codimension $1\le r\le n$, $\kappa=1-2\eta$ and $0\le\eta<1/2$. The label mechanism, ambient toggle convention and independent-noise assumption are part of the comparison class. The dimension, direction, offset and token-to-state updates are hidden. In the raw-stream experiment a fixed sequence of fair setup bits also permits every initial vector, and every finite sequence of named toggles has positive probability before a query.

**PROVED.** At a toggle boundary, two histories ending at $x,x'$ have identical forecasts after every permitted continuation exactly when $x+x'\in V$. Translation by any vector in $V$ fixes $F$. Otherwise continue by $x+a$ and query; precisely one of the resulting positions is in $F$. Thus any exact deterministic causal implementation, in any encoding and with unpriced prior history inaccessible, requires at least $2^r$ reachable states there. Choose any row basis $A$ of $V^\perp$. The state $s=Ax$ has $r$ bits, updates by XOR with the learned column $Ae_i$ at toggle $i$, and predicts the rare event at $s=Aa$. This attains the bound. The identified object is the quotient and its token action; invertible recoding of its state basis is observationally equivalent. For $r\ge2$ the same current “outside” forecast occurs on distinct cosets that respond differently to future inputs. At $r=1$ the current probability already distinguishes the two states. Other protocol phases and producing the full normalized vocabulary have their own costs.

**Passive learning has a sharp noise-dependent price.** Write $k=n-r$, $p=2^{-r}$ and $M=2^r{n\brack r}_2$ for the number of flats. The subspace count follows by counting ordered independent normal vectors and dividing by the number of bases of a fixed $r$-plane. Its product implies $r(k+1)\le\log_2 M\le r(k+1)-\sum_{j\ge1}\log_2(1-2^{-j})$; the last series is finite. With iid uniform $x$ independent of the generating flat, the minimax number of ordinary labels for exact recovery with fixed success probability at least $3/4$ has orders
$$
\Theta\!\big(2^r(k+1)\big)\quad(\eta=0),\qquad
\Theta_\eta\!\big(2^r r(k+1)\big)\quad(0<\eta<1/2\text{ fixed}).
$$

For the lower bounds use a uniform flat prior. At any fixed input, the binary information in one answer is $h(\eta+\kappa p)-h(\eta)$ nats, where $h$ is binary entropy with natural logarithms. The information in a whole passive sample is at most the sum of these quantities by conditional independence given the flat and inputs and subadditivity of entropy. At zero noise it is $h(p)\asymp p(r+1)$. At fixed positive noise it is at most $p\kappa\ln((1-\eta)/\eta)$ by concavity. Fano's inequality with $M$ equally likely possibilities gives the orders (the finitely small cases follow by a two-point test). The lower bound already gives the learner the true codimension.

In the clean case, retain the first positive origin; the differences of positive points are uniform on $V$ given the origin. After $k+u$ further positives the probability of failing to span is at most $(2^k-1)2^{-(k+u)}$, by a union over nonzero dual directions of $V$. Counting the ordinary draws needed to see these positives gives the fixed-confidence upper bound, including $k=0$. The learner can stop without the codimension. Wait for $s$ consecutive additional positives inside the current hull and reset the count on a dimension increase. From an incorrect hull of dimension $j<k$, this false stop before the next increase has probability $2^{-s(k-j)}$. Summing over possible stages gives at most $2^{-s}/(1-2^{-s})$. The expected number of positives to a full span, including the origin, is $1+\sum_{j=0}^{k-1}(1-2^{j-k})^{-1}\le k+2.607$. Allow another $s$ positives to confirm. Divide by $p$ to price every ordinary label. Gaussian elimination uses polynomial work in $n$ per positive and stores at most $O(n^2)$ basis bits; it also reads all the raw positions and labels.

For positive noise, two different codimension-$r$ flats disagree on at least a $p$ fraction of uniform inputs, because their intersection has size at most half of either. The affinity of their one-observation laws is at most $1-p(1-2\sqrt{\eta(1-\eta)})$. A false flat beating the true likelihood has probability at most the $m$-th power of this quantity at $m$ examples, by the square-root likelihood-ratio bound. Summing over candidates proves the noisy upper bound, with an extra $O_\eta(p^{-1}\ln(1/\delta))$ observations for confidence $1-\delta$. The fixed-rank MLE maximizes its signed count of observed labels inside the candidate. Searching all flats and Bayesian averaging, as in the finite test, can be expensive. Already $r=1$ contains passive learning parity with classification noise. A known subexponential algorithm for that problem supplies context for the computational difficulty; this reduction is not a proof of superpolynomial time. Repeating queries at the same chosen input changes the acquisition model. For positive noise, using the clean hull procedure on all positive-labelled points eventually fills the entire ambient space: the probability it still fits a proper hyperplane is at most $2(2^n-1)(1-\eta/2)^m$ after $m$ draws, by testing the complement of each affine hyperplane. [Blum, Kalai and Wasserman](https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/research/publication/noise-tolerant-learning-parity-problem-statistical-query-model/)

Exact recovery addresses a particular loss scale. The constant marginal has excess $h(\eta+\kappa p)-h(\eta)$ over the oracle, only $O_\eta(p)$ at positive noise. Conversely, let $R_F(q)$ be the mean excess binary log loss of any probabilistic head on uniform inputs. On a point where two flats disagree, their two target probabilities are $\eta,1-\eta$. Their mean excess is minimized at $q=1/2$, with value $\ln2-h(\eta)$. Hence $(R_F(q)+R_{F'}(q))/2\ge p[\ln2-h(\eta)]$ for distinct flats. A head below that threshold uniquely identifies the nearest flat under this criterion, so the exact-recovery sample lower bound also applies to unrestricted heads aiming for that accuracy. On a raw token stream the per-query loss must additionally be multiplied by its query-answer frequency.

A restricted initial feature dictionary can prevent even the first differentiable learning signal. For uniform inputs the indicator of $F$ has Walsh expansion $p\sum_{w\in V^\perp}(-1)^{w\cdot a}\chi_w(x)$. If no nonzero row of the dual occurs among a fixed set of parity features, population logistic loss on those features has zero gradient at the constant marginal and is convex in its coefficients. For a fixed linear synopsis $Bx$ with $\operatorname{row}(B)\cap V^\perp=\{0\}$, the joint linear maps have additive rank, so $Bx$ and $Ax$ are independent, and even an unrestricted decoder on that synopsis cannot improve the marginal. A uniform random dual contains any one specified nonzero feature with probability $(2^r-1)/(2^n-1)$. These claims price a supplied feature dictionary. The clean positive-hull learner instead constructs its own update directions from ordinary outcomes. [Raw acquisition, full-vocabulary loss and fitted executions](EVIDENCE.md#learning-a-state-update-from-a-rare-ordinary-token)

## An exact price for constructing several predictive bits together

Consider a delayed version of the flat query. Two **independently generated complete raw segments** produce uniform cumulative toggle vectors $X,Z\in\mathbb F_2^n$. Their paths, order, timing and external storage carry no cross-segment side information. A causal machine passes at most $b$ fixed-width bits of live state across the cut and updates them on the second segment. Its answer predicts $1_F(X+Z)$ with the preceding noise level. The source law and the flat may be known for the achievable loss calculation; learning them is priced separately above. Write $q=2^r$ and $m=2^b$ for $0\le b\le r$.

**PROVED.** Among *all* such causal states and probabilistic heads, the minimum per-query excess log loss above $h(\eta)$ is
$$
D_b=\frac{h(\eta+\kappa\,2^{b-r})-h(\eta)}{2^b}.
$$
Any $b$ independent coordinates of the learned $r$-bit syndrome attain the optimum with XOR updates on raw toggles. Further, the predictive gains $G_b=D_0-D_b$ have strictly increasing increments for $r\ge2$. At a constant price $\lambda\ge0$ per retained bit, an optimum is the complete $r$-bit computation when $\lambda<G_r/r$, or the constant predictor when $\lambda>G_r/r$. Exactly the two endpoints tie at equality for $r\ge2$. A learner that adds a single bit only when its immediate net gain is positive can therefore stop at zero when the complete learned computation would pay for itself.

First give the decoder the entire second segment, which can only improve its prediction. Write $U=AX$ and $W=AZ+Aa$. These are independent uniform elements of a set of $q$ values, and the desired event is $U=W$. Given $W$, the rest of the independent second path supplies no information about $U$ or the first message. Whatever other details of the first path enter the message now constitute a randomized encoder of $U$. For any decoder, each $U$ can instead be assigned deterministically to a message minimizing its own conditional expected loss; refitting the decoder cannot worsen this value. Shared source-independent randomness also cannot improve the minimum, by conditioning on it. It suffices to bound partitions of the $q$ values into at most $m$ cells.

For a nonempty cell of size $s$, a query $W$ in that cell has answer probability $\eta+\kappa/s$, and all others have probability $\eta$. Its contribution to excess is $g(s)/q^2$, where $g(s)=s^2[h(\eta+\kappa/s)-h(\eta)]$, taking $g(0)=0$. This cost is strictly convex on the positive integers in the sense of positive discrete second differences. For real $s\ge2$ put $t=\eta+\kappa/s\le1/2$. Direct differentiation gives
$$
g''(s)=2[h(t)-h(\eta)]-2(t-\eta)h'(t)+(t-\eta)^2h''(t)
      =\int_\eta^t(v-\eta)^2h'''(v)\,dv>0.
$$
Here $h'''(v)=(1-2v)/[v^2(1-v)^2]$; at $\eta=0$ the integrand has a finite endpoint limit. The remaining join is $g(3)-2g(2)+g(1)=C(\kappa)-9C(\kappa/3)>0$, where $C(z)=\ln2-h((1+z)/2)=\sum_{j\ge1}z^{2j}/[2j(2j-1)]$. The first series term cancels and every later one is positive, also at $\kappa=1$ by convergence. Finally $g(0)=g(1)=0$ and $g(2)>0$. Balancing any two cell sizes differing by at least two strictly reduces total cost. Thus the relaxed optimum for any message alphabet has sizes differing by at most one. When $m=2^b$ it has $m$ cells of size $q/m$, giving $D_b$.

The relaxation is attained online. Store any $b$ independent syndrome coordinates, XOR their learned mask on each incoming token and test the partial match after the second segment. A partial match has probability $1/m$ and conditional full-match probability $m/q$. The head releases the corresponding probability without retaining the raw second path. This proves optimality even over arbitrary nonlinear, path-dependent encodings of the same fixed width. For a non-power-of-two message alphabet the balanced-cell proof still bounds a decoder given the whole second segment; a matching online update needs its own construction.

To prove increasing increments, take a fixed full basis and let $B_i$ indicate agreement of the $i$-th syndrome coordinate with its comparison coordinate. These are independent fair bits and the response is their noisy conjunction. The optimum just found gives $G_b=I(Y;B_1,\ldots,B_b)$. Exchangeability and the conditional mutual information chain rule show that the increase in the successive marginal gains equals $I(B_{b+1};B_{b+2}\mid Y,B_1,\ldots,B_b)\ge0$. It is strictly positive for $b\le r-2$. Conditional on all earlier matches and on $Y=0$, the two next bits have an unnormalized likelihood table with three entries $1-\eta$ and a fourth $1-\eta-\kappa\,2^{-(r-b-2)}$. Its determinant is nonzero and the conditioning event has positive mass. The objective $G_b-\lambda b$ is consequently strictly convex as a sequence, so its maximum is at an endpoint.

At fixed $0<\eta<1/2$ and $p=1/q\to0$, the best first bit earns $\kappa^2p^2/[2\eta(1-\eta)]+O_\eta(p^3)$ while the complete state earns $\kappa\ln((1-\eta)/\eta)p+O_\eta(p^2)$. For $\eta=1/8,r=8$, the first bit earns .00003847 nats, all eight earn .00566193, and the complete-state break-even price is .00070774 per bit per query. For instance $.0003$ per bit makes every one-bit addition from the constant predictor unprofitable, while buying all eight improves the priced objective by .00326193 nats. This is an obstruction to purely one-at-a-time improvement, including the best unrestricted one-bit causal encoding. The resource is fixed-width state at a specified cut. Prices for variable-length codes, rare writes, offline training, a different continuation law or per-token rather than per-query forecasting require a different calculation. In particular, independent cumulative vectors alone are insufficient: a suffix encoder could append two cancelling copies of a chosen token to signal the answer in its ordering. [Small exact partition comparisons](EVIDENCE.md#learning-a-state-update-from-a-rare-ordinary-token)

**Rigidity of the learned computation.** Assume in addition that the first segment gives every finite named-toggle word positive probability and the machine is deterministic, time-homogeneous, starts from a fixed state, and has no special rewrite or external memory at the cut. Then for every $0\le b\le r$, **every** machine attaining $D_b$ implements $b$ independent linear functionals of the true syndrome, up to an arbitrary permutation of its internal state names. The state updates and the hidden input directions are conclusions about the attained predictor, even when the competing transition and readout functions were arbitrary.

To prove necessity, form $P(M\mid U)$ from the first segment. At a globally optimal decoder this encoder is a mixture over deterministic selectors: independently choose one supported message at each $u$. Every such selector has positive mixture weight and costs at least the already proved optimum, even after refitting its head. Since their mean at the fixed decoder attains the optimum, every selector attains it. Every optimal deterministic selector has all $m$ cell sizes exactly $q/m$. If one $u$ could send two messages, select identically on all other $u$ and switch just this one; the two resulting size vectors cannot both be balanced. Thus $M=J(U)$ deterministically. Every first word has positive probability, so this equality holds on each word and its one-letter extensions. Homogeneity implies $J(u+Ae_i)=T_iJ(u)$ for every $u$ and every named toggle. The columns $Ae_i$ generate the syndrome group. Consequently equal cells remain equal under every translation, with the reverse implication obtained by applying the same translation again. The cell containing zero is a subspace and all cells are its cosets. Its index is $2^b$. Choosing any basis of its annihilator gives the claimed linear bits and the conjugate XOR updates. Positive word support is feasible at finite expected length: generate a full-support geometric-length word, draw the desired uniform cumulative vector independently, and append canonical toggles to reach it. Any given word can occur followed by an empty correction. Generate the two segments independently. Without full word/extension support, transitions outside the training support can differ arbitrarily. [Lean verification of the translation-consistency and future-hit steps](CausalState.lean)

**Variable message length has an exact separate bound.** For a deterministic partition let $\phi(s)=g(s)/s$. The function $\phi(e^t)$ is strictly concave. For $z=\kappa/s$ and $H(z)=h(\eta+z)-h(\eta)$ its second derivative in $t$ is
$$
\kappa\left\{z^{-1}\int_0^z v[-h''(\eta+v)]\,dv-z[-h''(\eta+z)]\right\}<0.
$$
Indeed $v/[(\eta+v)(1-\eta-v)]$ is strictly increasing, since the numerator of its derivative is $\eta(1-\eta)+v^2$. At zero noise use continuity at the endpoints. Comparing to the chord on $1\le s\le q$ gives $\phi(s)\ge\phi(q)\ln(s)/\ln(q)$. Summing the partition loss now yields $D\ge D_0 H(U\mid M)/\ln q$, hence $G\le G_r H(M)/\ln q$ for deterministic messages. For stochastic encoders the gain $I(Y;M\mid W)=I(Y,W;M)$ is convex in the encoder while $H(M)$ is concave, preserving the same upper bound via deterministic mixtures. A self-delimiting message of expected length $B$ bits therefore earns at most $G_r\min(1,B/r)$. Public time-sharing, independently agreed before seeing the data, between a full $r$-bit syndrome and zero bits attains this line for any expected budget $0\le B\le r$. Prefix decodability holds conditional on the public mode. A private decision to transmit, an uncharged end-of-stream signal, and cost-free knowledge of the public mode are different interfaces. The bound uses message entropy, which can exceed the information the message contains about the syndrome. For $r=1$ with a fair noisy one-bit encoder of correlation $\alpha$, these two mutual informations have ratio $C(\kappa\alpha)/C(\alpha)\to\kappa^2$ as $\alpha\to0$, which at $\eta=1/8$ exceeds $C(\kappa)/\ln2$. The original OpenAI cube-contraction paper studies a one-bit summary against a full vector of independently noised input coordinates; our observer receives a translated equality query with one noisy answer. Its scalar entropy function is shared, while the two observation laws and proofs differ. [Original manuscript and complete reading](../../literature/notes/OAI-1006-237.md)

These price and rigidity results condition on the true flat for deployment. Before any labels from a uniformly unknown flat, every input has the same predictive marginal. The clean acquisition proof constructs the required dual directions from ordinary outcomes; the noisy likelihood proof provides a sample bound and a finite procedure whose computation can be large. The priced existence and necessity of an internal state alone do not imply that a specified optimizer reaches it.

## Inferring a token action from ordinary continuation responses

Let $f(w)\in\{0,1\}$ specify which of two distinct conditional answer probabilities follows a finite raw word. The input letters are uninterpreted. Define two prefixes to have the same state exactly when their answers agree after every common continuation. Suppose this minimal reachable deterministic Moore machine has $q$ states. A complete input word can be submitted and only its ordinary final answer observed. There is no oracle producing counterexamples or exposing the hidden states. This is a different acquisition interface from classical learning with both membership and equivalence queries. [Angluin, *Learning Regular Sets from Queries and Counterexamples*](https://homepages.math.uic.edu/~lreyzin/papers/angluin87.pdf)

**PROVED.** On this minimal machine, the input maps all commute precisely when $f(uabv)=f(ubav)$ for every prefix $u$, suffix $v$ and pair of letters $a,b$. Each squares to the identity precisely when $f(uaav)=f(uv)$ for all $u,v,a$. One direction follows by applying the equal transitions. For the converse, reach any state by $u$ and compare all suffixes $v$ after the two candidate transitions. Equality of their continuation rows is equality of minimal states. The conclusions concern the observable predictive machine. A larger hidden implementation may have arbitrary extra distinctions.

If both laws hold, count each of the $d$ raw letters modulo two. Any word can be sorted by adjacent swaps and repeated equal letters deleted. The induced elementary abelian group acts transitively on all reachable states. A group element fixing the initial state fixes the entire orbit because it commutes with every transition. Hence after quotienting the raw parity group $\mathbb F_2^d$ by its stabilizer the action is free and transitive. Equivalently, if $g$ is the binary response on canonical parity words and $H=\{v:g(x+v)=g(x)\ \forall x\}$, the predictive states are exactly the cosets of $H$. There are $2^{d-\dim H}$, and every raw letter updates this group by addition of its learned element. The output coloring in the quotient can be arbitrary with trivial translation stabilizer. For a nonconstant coloring, the high set of raw parities is an affine flat exactly when one minimal state is high: an affine flat has its direction as its full translation stabilizer, while one high coset is itself affine.
